- **Why:** the owner took up D-101's Later list: the blind test's most common complaint, generic modern mid-rises; machiya rows OSM maps as one footprint, drawn as one merged roof; no wires between the utility poles; and no shapes for Kinkaku-ji, Nijō Castle or the Imperial Palace.
- **What:**
  - **Kyoto's mid-rises** (`midrise`): a pent eave of tile over the ground floor with the storeys above set back a voxel, as Kyoto's landscape rules ask (45% of `building.midrise`); vertical louvres before an office's glass; an air conditioner's box on some flats' balconies; an old water tank on some roofs. Walls in beige, white and grey tile and concrete: in the blind test the brown tile read as American brick ("New York, Midtown South"), so brown is gone from the mid-rise and commercial groups.
  - **Rows split into houses** (`machiya`): a footprint wider than two houses (`unit`, 0.8 of the height) is a row: each house its own wall height (a voxel up or down), tiles, door, lattice and slits, paired pillars at the party walls, plaster where a taller house's gable stands over its neighbour's roof.
  - **Wires** (`utilityPole`, `span`, `sag`): two power lines from the crossarm and a telecom cable, reaching half the spacing to each side and sagging toward mid-span, where the next pole's meet them. A street-prop option `along` (pipeline) puts one row on one side of a road and turns each prop to run with it; the poles use it (1,265 on the walking level, where they were 2,301 on both sides).
  - **Kinkaku-ji** (`goldenPavilion`, `landmark.pavilion.gold`, Wikidata's point, 12.5 m drawn 1.5×): a first storey of natural wood and white panels under a thin shingle pent roof, a gilded second storey under a hipped shingle roof, a small gilded third under a curved pyramid, a gilt phoenix.
  - **Nijō Castle and the Imperial Palace** (driving level): their halls and gates typed by name (`building.palace`, tile; `building.palace.gosho`, cypress bark), with `from` so a hall drawn in parts (the 紫宸殿) is typed whole; `templeHall` draws them with white walls between dark pillars under shallow eaves. PLATEAU has no models of the Imperial Palace's buildings, and gives Nijō's halls the code of temples and schools (422) with gabled roofs, which had drawn them as houses.
  - **Pinnacles:** a hall's gable ornament and raised ridge ends only on halls of some size; on narrow corridors they stood in rows "like Gothic pinnacles".
- **Measured** (builds of 2026-11-11): Nijō 33 pieces and the Imperial Palace 32 typed by name; the walking level 2.1 MB, the driving 7.5 MB, the flying 392 KB, all road reports clean. Tests: the row's heights and party walls, the pent eave, the pavilion's storeys and phoenix, the wires' sag, `along`'s one side and turns.
- **Blind test** (a fresh evaluator, five new views): Nijō Castle 65% ("the Ninomaru Palace and the moated inner enclosure"), the Imperial Palace 65%, Kinkaku-ji 40% (the pavilion brown at that scale, its pond drawn as sand), a temple compound in Gion as Kyoto 45%; the weakest, Shijō-Karasuma's office blocks, as New York at 25%: what led to the paler palette above. That view wasn't tested again after the change.
- **Rejected alternatives:**
  - **Splitting rows in the pipeline** (cutting footprints at plausible party walls): every later step would see more, smaller buildings and lose the footprint's facts' match; the generator knows its front and draws the row.
  - **Wires as props of their own between poles:** props stand alone and don't know their neighbours; a pole drawing half of each span meets the next one's.
  - **The Imperial Palace's unnamed buildings by the park they stand in:** the Gyoen is one `park` among many; only names single out the palace.
- **Later:** the Imperial Palace's covered corridors (unnamed) still draw as low modern blocks, and its courts and the park as lawn where they are gravel; Nijō's stone walls and corner towers; Kinkaku-ji's pond (ESA reads it as bare ground); wires follow quarter turns, so on a diagonal street they run off the road's line.

### D-103: Ponds and moats from Overture's water areas, a park's courts left paved, wires on diagonal streets (accepted, M5)
- **Why:** the owner asked to address what of D-102's Later list the data can detect. Lakes came only from ESA WorldCover's water class and rivers only from Overture's lines, so Kinkaku-ji's pond read as bare ground and Nijō Castle's moats were patchy; the Imperial Palace's gravel courts were lawn because the Gyoen park is; wires ran off diagonal streets because props turn only by quarters.
- **What the data has:** Overture's water areas in the driving box: 269 ponds (Kinkaku-ji's 鏡湖池, 6,230 m²), two moats (Nijō's 外濠, 29,477 m², and 内濠, 16,880 m²), reservoirs; also pools and wastewater tanks, left out. Land use maps the palace's courts as `pedestrian` areas inside the park. Every road's direction.
- **What:**
  - **`sources.lakes`** (pipeline, opt-in): Overture's water areas of the given classes are still water beside land cover's; each body is levelled as a lake (one level per body, `minLakeArea` applies). Kyoto's three levels take `[pond, moat, lake, reservoir]`: 199 cells on the walking level, 291 on the driving, 95 on the flying.
  - **`ground.lawn.paved`** (pipeline): land-use classes inside a park that a lawn leaves as they are; Kyoto's levels name `pedestrian`, so the Imperial Palace's courts and the parks' plazas stay paved grey.
  - **`diagonal`** on street props with `along` (pipeline): the group placed where a road runs within 22.5° of a diagonal, turned for its diagonal; `utilityPole`'s `diagonal` draws the crossarm and wires along x = y. On the walking level 109 of 1,265 poles stand on diagonal streets.
  - **The Imperial Palace's corridors:** a rule for narrow (3–7 m), long (25 m or more) and low buildings in a park types 8 pieces as palace corridors.
- **Measured** (builds of 2026-11-11): road reports clean; the walking level 2.1 MB, the driving 7.5 MB, the flying 392 KB. Tests: the water areas' cells, a park's paved courts, the diagonal group and its turns, the diagonal pole.
- **Looked at:** Kinkaku-ji's pavilion on its pond's shore; both of Nijō Castle's moats round the Honmaru and the Ninomaru; the Imperial Palace's courts in grey between its halls.
- **Rejected alternatives:**
  - **Every Overture water class:** swimming pools (96) and wastewater tanks (63) would be lakes on roofs and in plants.
  - **River polygons as lakes:** a river is one body, and levelling one along its length would cut terraces into its valley; rivers stay lines.
  - **Props turned by eighths:** the engine turns instances by quarters; a second group drawn on the diagonal needs no engine change.
- **Later:** the Imperial Palace's main corridors are mapped as one ring round each court, so no rule on width finds them (a building's holes, D-088's courtyards, could); wires on the 16 directions' 2:1 streets still run a little off; Nijō's stone walls show only where the terrain drops to the moats.

### D-104: Walls from OSM's barrier lines, corridors by how little they fill, wires on the 2:1 streets (accepted, M5)
- **Why:** the owner asked to finish D-103's Later list: the Imperial Palace's corridors, still low modern blocks; Nijō Castle's stone walls, which showed only where the terrain drops to the moats; and wires running off the 16 directions' 2:1 streets.
- **What the data has:** the palace's corridors are not rings with holes but thin C-shaped polygons round each court, filling 4–42% of their smallest enclosing rectangle (one is 1,757 m² of a 44,368 m² rectangle). OSM maps 583 retaining walls (24 km) in the driving box, 51 of them (6.7 km) round Nijō Castle's moats: its ishigaki.
- **What:**
  - **`maxFill`**, a typology rule field (pipeline): the most of its smallest enclosing rectangle a footprint fills. Kyoto's driving level types low (to 12 m), long (25 m or more) buildings in a park that fill under half of it as the palace's corridors: 64 pieces of the Imperial Palace are now typed, 32 more than in D-103. Its outer earthen wall is one of them, and reads as a wall under a little roof.
  - **`objects.walls`** (pipeline): segments of a wall one `spacing` apart along Overture's barrier lines of the given classes, turned with each line; they stand over water's edge and cliffs, which street props keep off, but not on buildings. Kyoto's driving level puts `stoneWall` (`wall.stone`: two voxels thick, three tall, a cap course) on the retaining walls every 12.5 m: 1,944 segments, on the 814 pieces of line the build reads, Nijō's moats lined with grey ramparts. The flying level turns them off.
  - **`slants`** beside `diagonal` (pipeline): with `along`, two groups for the 2:1 directions (26.6° and 63.4° from east), each turned a quarter for the direction at right angles; `along_turn` picks the nearest of the 16 directions modulo a half turn, falling back to the nearest group there is. `utilityPole` and `stoneWall` draw their line along any of (0, 1), (1, 1), (2, −1), (1, −2) (`wx`, `wy`). On the walking level 1,054 poles are straight, 39 diagonal and 172 on 2:1 streets; the driving level's walls are 1,451 straight, 158 diagonal and 335 on 2:1 lines.
- **One-pixel props (D-099), on merging:** Kyoto's street lights and signals take the line options every pack has, as on the other 10 m walking levels: lights 16 voxels tall with a 3-voxel arm, signals 12 with a 5-voxel arm. Its utility poles keep their own voxel arms and wires (`utilityPole`), not Tokyo's line spans.
- **Measured** (builds of 2026-11-11): road reports clean; the walking level 2.1 MB, the driving 7.5 MB, the flying 392 KB. Tests: `maxFill` on a block and a C, the walls' placement on barrier lines over water, `along_turn` for all eight directions both ways and its fallbacks, the wall segments and the 2:1 pole's cable.
- **Looked at:** the Imperial Palace's halls joined by cypress-bark galleries round its paved courts; Nijō Castle's two moats lined with stone; wires along the Higashiyama lanes.
- **Rejected alternatives:**
  - **Courtyards (holes, D-088) for the corridors:** OSM doesn't map them as rings with holes.
  - **Walls as line landmarks:** each is a hand-placed line in the registry; OSM's lines are already there.
  - **Every barrier class:** plain walls (1,399 of them, 48 km) and fences would wall in every garden; retaining walls are the stone ones.
- **Later:** the walking level's retaining walls (its voxel is smaller, so its wall groups would need their own span); the palace's few corridor blocks that fill more than half their rectangle; props turned by the 16 directions in the engine would make the four groups one.

### D-105: Releases from CI through npm trusted publishing, and every city pack published (accepted)
- **Why:** the owner asked to set up publishing for the open-source release. Publishing was by hand from a maintainer's machine (D-074), and npm is ending direct publishing with granular tokens (January 2027); trusted publishing (OIDC) needs no stored token at all.
- **What:**
  - **`.github/workflows/release.yml`**, changesets' trusted-publishing workflow: on main, pending changesets open or update the "Version packages" pull request (`pnpm release:version`: `changeset version`, then the version pins); merging it packs the packages whose versions aren't on npm, checks the tarballs (`pnpm package:check`), and publishes them with the npm CLI's OIDC exchange, with tags and GitHub releases. Only the publish job gets `id-token: write`.
  - **All five city packs public:** `@pixelworlds/city-nyc`, `-paris`, `-kyoto` and `-tokyo` lose `private` and get a first-release changeset each. `tools/city-pack-check.mjs` checks any number of city pack tarballs (without arguments, it packs every `packages/city-*`); CI's package job packs and checks them all.
  - **The first release of each package is by hand** (docs/npm.md §9): npm attaches a trusted publisher only to a package that exists.
- **Rejected alternatives:**
  - **An npm token in the repository's secrets:** a long-lived credential to leak, and granular tokens stop publishing directly in January 2027.
  - **Staged publishing:** changesets doesn't support it yet; merging the version pull request is the human step.
- **Later:** consumer examples for the other city packs (`tools/consumers.mjs` covers San Francisco's); provenance, which npm adds only from a public repository.

### D-106: Kyoto's wires strung pole to pole in one-pixel lines at any angle, one pole group (accepted, M5)
- **Why:** Kyoto's poles drew their wires as voxels inside each pole's model (D-102), reaching half the spacing to each side to meet the next pole's. A prop turns by quarter turns, so the wires lined up only along the directions a model was drawn for: four pole groups (`prop.pole`, `.diagonal`, `.slant`, `.slant2`; D-103, D-104), still a little off on streets between those directions, and broken wherever a lane bent or the next pole stood nearer or further than one spacing. Tokyo's wire spans (D-098) are line objects from one pole to the next, drawn in one-pixel lines at any angle.
- **What:**
  - **`wireSpan`** (`@pixelworlds/city-tokyo`) gains `across` and `cable`: with `across`, two lines in place of one, that many voxels to either side of the poles along the cell axis nearer square to the span (the axis a pole's crossarm lies on, the poles turning by quarter turns); with `cable`, one more over the poles at that height. At their defaults Tokyo's single wire is drawn as before.
  - **`utilityPole`** (`@pixelworlds/city-kyoto`) is the pole alone: concrete, a steel crossarm across x with an insulator at each end, a transformer on some, 16 voxels tall on every pole (it was 16 ± 10%) so the spans meet its crossarm. Its `span`, `sag`, `wx` and `wy` and the diagonal and 2:1 models are gone.
  - **The `kyoto` pack** names Tokyo's plugin too, as Tokyo's names San Francisco's, for `prop.wire`: `wireSpan` with the power lines 15.5 voxels up and 2 to either side (the insulators' middles), the telecom cable at 11.5 on the pole, sagging 2 voxels, in `timber-dark`. One pole group, `prop.pole`.
  - **`kyoto-walk`:** the two pole rules take `wires: prop.wire` in place of `along`, `diagonal` and `slants`: a pole every 30 m along whole streets (`objects._strokes`, D-098), on the left 3 m off the lanes' centre lines and 5 m off the tertiary roads', and a span between each two. The pipeline's `along`, `diagonal` and `slants` stay for the driving level's stone walls.
- **Measured** (build of 2026-11-11): 3,650 poles and 3,002 spans, where there were 1,265 poles (1,054 straight, 39 diagonal, 172 on 2:1 streets): `along` placed poles per Overture segment from half a spacing in, and Kyoto's lanes are cut into short segments at every junction, so most short blocks had none; along whole streets every 30 m of lane has one. 62,968 objects; the package grows from 2.1 to 2.2 MB; the build takes 69 s. The fixture was last built before the merge into `nyc-walk`, so the rebuild also brings in that merge's ground (D-094 to D-096); a rebuild of the previous spec gives the same chunks as this one. Looked at under the Yasaka Pagoda and on the Higashiyama lanes at zooms 2 and 3, Nakagyō, and Gion turned a quarter: three dark lines a pixel wide from crossarm to crossarm at each lane's own angle, sagging between the poles. Tests: the pole's model and its insulators where the pack's spans meet them; a span's pair and cable, apart along the axis nearer square to it, at three angles. The Yasaka golden is regenerated; every other golden is unchanged.
- **Rejected alternatives:**
  - **Props turned by the 16 directions in the engine** (D-104's Later): one pole group, but wires drawn in the pole's model still end half a spacing out, missing the next pole wherever the spacing isn't even or the lane bends.
  - **A Kyoto copy of `wireSpan`:** the same generator in two packs; a pack naming another's plugin is how Tokyo's takes San Francisco's.
  - **One line per span, as Tokyo's:** Kyoto's poles carry two power lines and a cable (D-102); one wire over the crossarm's middle reads as Tokyo's.

### D-107: The global path's first pieces: Copernicus GLO-30 terrain with a ground filter, and facts from neighbourhood boxes (accepted, M5)
- **Why:** the owner asked for a Kowloon pilot on open data while the Lands Department is asked for a grant (docs/cities.md, Hong Kong). Hong Kong's own buildings and DTM can't be adapted under their terms, so the pilot needs what docs/cities.md lists for the global path: a terrain source that works anywhere, and typology without a records dataset.
- **What was measured first** (2026-11-11, Sheung Wan to Sham Shui Po, against LandsD's Building layer and 5 m DTM, used only as the reference and kept out of the repo): open footprints cover 80% of LandsD's building area; OSM floors are within 3 floors on 60% of the buildings that have them; buildings without them are guesses, and the pipeline's 6–8 m default draws 20–40 m tong lau at 0.38× their height; raw GLO-30 has an RMSE of 12.8 m on streets and reads flat streets 3.8 m high. The numbers are in docs/cities.md.
- **What:**
  - **`copernicus-glo30`**, a built-in terrain source: Copernicus DEM GLO-30's 1°×1° COGs on AWS (anonymous), overviews to 8×. Attribution as its licence asks for adapted data, with the no-liability sentence it says to pass on.
  - **`copernicus-glo30-ground`**: the same with buildings and trees taken off by a grey opening over a 210 m window on GLO-30's own 1″ grid (water and gaps left alone, so the shore isn't pulled down), then a 3×3 mean, before it's warped onto the region. Over the Kowloon pilot, on all land cells against LandsD's DTM: an RMSE of 6.4 m instead of 9.4 m, a median of +0.4 m instead of +3.6 m, and 8% of cells off by more than 10 m instead of 24%. On slopes over 15° it is no better than raw (15.4 against 13.4 m): for flat dense cities, not hillsides.
  - **The `areas` facts source**, built in: facts written in the spec, by neighbourhood box (`objects.typology.facts: { plugin: areas, areas: [{ hood, bounds, floors, use }] }`; first match wins). A building in an area gets its `hood` (for `hoods:` rules) and, where Overture gives no height or floors, the area's typical height (`floors` × 3.2 m) through the facts' `height` (D-084). Kowloon's floors are the median of OSM's floors on footprints under 300 m² in each area. Against LandsD, a box default is within 3 floors on 61% of the buildings OSM has no floors for.
- **Rejected alternatives:**
  - **3D-GloBFP heights** (CC BY 4.0, a height per footprint): it pulls heights toward the middle (low buildings 1.4×, 40–100 m blocks 0.75×, towers 0.46×), and calibrated against OSM's floors it was no better than a box default. Not wired in.
  - **FABDEM** (bare-earth GLO-30): CC BY-NC-SA. **A wider window** (330 m): better on flat streets (7.0 m) but worse on slopes (19.4 m).
  - **A spec option for the window** instead of a second kind: one window has been measured; a second city can add one.
- **Later:** a ground filter that reads the roads (the lowest GLO-30 along each street) for hillside cities; the window's effect at coarser levels (at 4× overview it shrinks to 3 pixels; at 8× it's off).

### D-108: Hong Kong's look: a `hongkong` style pack and the `@pixelworlds/city-hongkong` generator plugin (accepted, M5)
- **Why:** Kowloon's walking level (Mong Kok, Yau Ma Tei, Jordan, Tsim Sha Tsui and West Kowloon at 10 m, built from open data alone) reads by a fabric no pack draws: rows of tong lau whose fronts are balconies of laundry and air conditioners over shops under signboards, slender composite buildings gridded with small windows, pencil towers of bay windows, and the neon signs that hang out over Nathan Road and the side streets from both sides. Tokyo's zakkyo stands its sign boards in front of its own wall; no pack hangs a sign over the street.
- **What:**
  - **The style pack** `worlds/_styles/hongkong`, a full pack laid out like `tokyo` (D-092), with 41 ramps: the tong lau's faded mint, yellow, salmon, cream and sky blue and white mosaic tile, the composites' grey and beige tile, concrete, granite, brick, grime and rust, flat roofs, steel, glass and blue-green curtain glass, the neon red, pink, cyan, yellow, green and white (they glow at night with the lit windows and the marker, and nothing else does), a dark asphalt, pale concrete pavements, the harbour's deep green-blue, Kowloon's red taxis, the Star Ferry's jade, banyans (style guide §23). It names `["@pixelworlds/city-sf", "@pixelworlds/city-hongkong"]`: San Francisco's `block` for civic blocks and flatted factories and `sfTower` for the commercial glass towers. The generic groups the pipeline names before its typology rules (`building.residential`, `.commercial`, `.parking`, and `building.tower` above the spec's `towerMeters`) all have Hong Kong ramps.
  - **The plugin** `packages/city-hongkong`, wired as the other packs are (core's `KNOWN_PLUGIN_GENERATORS`, the viewer's `CITY_PACKS`, the game's plugins, the city-pack check's test, a changeset for core and one for its first npm release, public as D-105 made every pack):
    - `tongLau`: a footprint wider than a lot (7 voxels) is a row of houses, each its own colour and on some a storey taller or lower; a shop under its signboard, its shutter down or half up on some, the stair's gate; at every storey a balcony painted flush: the slab's edge, a railing (a light parapet, balusters or a steel grille), the balcony's shadow with a door's glass, laundry and an air conditioner whose drips stain the rail, or the balcony closed in with windows; laundry poles as one-pixel lines (D-098) out of some; a parapet with the party walls' piers above it, a roof hut, a tank on a stand, an aerial.
    - `compositeTower`: shops under signboards, billboards across the mezzanine on most, then a dense grid of small windows (pairs, ribbons or slots) with air conditioners and their drips; with `mall: 1` a mall or podium under big sign bands (`building.podium`). It is also the generic `building.residential` and `.commercial`, with fewer or more signs.
    - `pencilTower`: bay windows and air conditioners' ledges on every face, a glass lobby, a crown of fins round the water tanks' louvred enclosure (`building.tower.residential`).
    - `neonSign` (`prop.neon`, `prop.neon.wide`): one to three boards from each building line, staggered along the street and in height (6 to 20 m up), a voxel thick, wide or tall, a neon field with a tube border and speckled characters in another colour, each on a steel frame of one-pixel lines from its wall; the street's middle stays clear. Placed across the road (`across: true`), so the model's ends are the two building lines: `span` is 48 voxels (30 m) for Nathan Road and 28 (17.5 m) for the side streets, from Overture's buildings in Mong Kok, which stand 29–34 m apart across Nathan Road (median 31), 16–22 m across Shanghai and Reclamation Streets and 10–20 m across Sai Yeung Choi Street South, Dundas and Shantung Streets (where they don't open onto a square).
    - `curvedRoofHall` and `grandHotel`, two landmark shapes drawn on their footprints.
  - **Landmarks** (`worlds/_landmarks/`, generic; each replaces Overture's building by id): the International Commerce Centre (`landmark.tower.glass`, core's `obeliskTower`: 484 m, its footprint's centroid 114.160226 E 22.303378 N, 60 m across at its foot); the Tsim Sha Tsui Clock Tower (`landmark.tower.clock`, core's `towerHall`: 44 m, a line landmark 8 m long and thick through Overture's footprint at 114.169538 E 22.293593 N, so the kit draws the shaft on a band as wide); the Hong Kong Cultural Centre (`landmark.hall.curved`, on Overture's footprint, 132 by 116 m, centroid 114.170339 E 22.293814 N, at a depicted 50 m: Overture has no height for it); The Peninsula (`landmark.hotel`, on the 1928 hotel's footprint, 88 by 72 m, 40 m, centroid 114.171773 E 22.295247 N, and replacing the 1994 tower's too, 115 m, which it draws across the back of the main block).
  - **Helpers copied, not imported:** the footprint and paint helpers are Tokyo's (`facade.ts`, after New York's), as Tokyo copied New York's: a pack publishes its generators, not its helpers, and a published entry imports nothing but `pixelworld`.
- **Checked:** tests build every Hong Kong building group over footprints from 1 × 4 to 40 × 20, a notched lot and a diagonal, at heights of 10, 40 and 160 voxels, with and without hints (378 models: inside the footprint, no overhangs, glass only on the skin, the pack's ramps only), and check a wide tong lau's houses in several colours and heights, its signboard and a railing under the shadow or windows at every storey, its laundry poles out of the front, its roof huts; the composite's grid of glass and its drip stains; the mall's sign bands; the pencil tower's glass on all four faces and its enclosure over the crown; the curved hall's roof rising and steepening toward the back without glass; the hotel's tower over its wings; and the neon boards: on both sides of every model, within reach of their walls and never against them, between their heights, the middle clear, a voxel thick, each frame's lines from a wall. Looked at in the model preview by day and night and from every side.
- **Rejected alternatives:**
  - **Signs placed `along` the road:** one side only, and a model can't tell which of its sides the building is on (Overture's segments run either way), so an arm would point into the street half the time; `across` puts both building lines at the model's ends.
  - **Signs as part of the buildings:** they overhang the street, which the building rules forbid (§13); as props they hang from the walls' line.
  - **A generator of ICC's own:** `obeliskTower` already reads as its tapering glass shaft; only its top's rounded corners differ.
  - **Double-decker buses and minibuses:** core's `bus` is one deck at the size the traffic simulation gives it; the pack colours it.
- **Later:** double-deckers and green minibuses as vehicle types; ICC's square top and its light show; tall vertical signs on the tong lau themselves; the Cultural Centre's height and roof from survey data; signs on diagonal streets turn by quarter turns only.

### D-109: Kowloon's walking level, on open data alone (accepted, M5)
- **Why:** the owner chose a Kowloon pilot on open data (D-107) while the Lands Department is asked for a grant; Hong Kong is the densest high-rise city on its candidate list, and docs/cities.md measured what open data loses there.
- **What:** `worlds/hongkong/walk.yaml` (`hongkong-walk`): Kowloon from Tsim Sha Tsui's waterfront to Prince Edward, West Kowloon and the ICC to King's Park, at 10 m per cell (228 × 444 cells), on the `hongkong` pack (D-108).
  - **Terrain:** `copernicus-glo30-ground` (D-107): 18 drawn steps, the harbour below.
  - **Heights:** OSM's floors where it has them; elsewhere the `areas` facts source gives each of eight neighbourhoods its typical height, the median of OSM's floors on footprints under 300 m² there (Jordan 5 storeys, Yau Ma Tei 6, Mong Kok 8, Tsim Sha Tsui 9; 3,328 buildings).
  - **Types**, by floors, footprint width, OSM's class and neighbourhood (`hoods:`), with `towerMeters` at 80 so the rules sort everything below 25 storeys: 3,726 tong lau (3–9 storeys up to 18 m wide in the old districts and Tsim Sha Tsui's back streets), 985 composite buildings (10–24 storeys up to 35 m wide), 299 residential towers (flats by OSM's class, or narrow ones in the old districts), 65 podiums and malls (up to 7 storeys, 40 m wide or more), 105 civic buildings; 61 glass towers and 7 hotels; 857 commercial and 995 residential keep the defaults.
  - **Neon:** signs hung across the streets from both sides (`across`, D-108): Nathan Road's 30 m span every 12 m (597), and the old districts' secondary, tertiary and residential streets, every 12 m within a box (5,075). A new street-row key, **`notName`**, keeps the narrower row off Nathan Road where it is tagged secondary.
  - **Landmarks (generic depictions):** the ICC, the Tsim Sha Tsui clock tower, the Cultural Centre and the Peninsula, each on its Overture footprint.
- **Measured** (build of 2026-11-11): 7,104 buildings, 23,730 objects; road report clean; 1.2 MB; built in 49 s from the download cache (110 s on a first build). Against LandsD's data (docs/cities.md), heights are about 2 floors off and the ground about 6 m (RMSE over the pilot's land).
- **Looked at:** Yau Ma Tei's and Jordan's pastel tong lau under neon; Mong Kok's towers along Nathan Road, and in the game the tower in front of the car seen through to the signs (D-079); the Cultural Centre's pink curved hall on the waterfront; the ICC over West Kowloon's roads.
- **Rejected alternatives:** signs on one side only (`along`): Overture's segments run either way, so half the arms would point into the street. Signs on every road in the box: West Kowloon's expressways and the park roads would carry them.
- **Later:** the driving and flying levels and Hong Kong Island (the Mid-Levels need a better ground than D-107's); a blind test; towers on podiums, which open data draws as one block (27% of footprints, docs/cities.md); double-decker buses and trams; LandsD's data if the grant comes.

### D-110: Every data source selected by role in the spec; the Python city packs merged into the pipeline (accepted)
- **Why:** the owner asked for one PyPI package, and for data sources a client can select or add rather than ones hard-wired into the pipeline. The four Python city packs (D-068, D-080, D-084, D-089) had the same dependencies as the pipeline, so splitting them saved nothing to install and added four releases. And only terrain, land cover and facts were chosen by name: Overture (roads, paths, rails, rivers, water, land use, barriers, buildings, transit), the canopy map, USGS lidar and Wikidata were imported directly, Overture's release read at ten places.
- **What:**
  - **Roles:** every source has one: `terrain`, `landcover`, `features`, `facts`, `canopy`, `heights`, `importance`. Each role's contract is a base class where it is consumed: `RasterSource`, `FeatureSource` (sources/features.py, new), `FactsSource`, `CanopySource`, `HeightSource`, `ImportanceSource`, all on `plugins.Source` (`role`, `attribution`, `options_schema`, `summary`).
  - **The spec:** `sources.<key>: { kind, …options }` for each role: `dem`, `landcover`, `features`, `facts`, `canopy`, `heights`, `importance`. `canopy` (`meta-chm`), `heights` (`usgs-3dep-lidar`) and `importance` (`wikidata`) have defaults. Keys moved: `sources.overture.release` to `sources.features: { kind: overture, release }`; `objects.typology.facts` to `sources.facts` (the older form, options beside a bare name, is gone); `objects.buildings.lidar.project` to `sources.heights`. The world specs are migrated.
  - **Opening:** a name stands for an instance (used as it is, no options) or a class, opened as `cls(region, options)`. Options are the block without `kind` and the pipeline's own keys (`overview`, `resampling`). A source's `options_schema` lets `pixelworld check` check them before anything is fetched; an instance given options is an error there too.
  - **The registry:** one entry point group, `pixelworld.sources` (`pixelworld.facts` is gone); a name used for the wrong role is an error that lists the names of the right one. `pixelworld plugins` lists sources by role.
  - **The city packs:** `datasf`, `pluto`, `apur`, `ign-rgealti`, `ign-lidar-hd-mnt`, `plateau` and `gsi-dem` are built in (`pipeline/src/pixelworld_pipeline/sources/`); `pipeline/plugins/` and the uv workspace are gone. PyPI has one distribution, `pixelworld-pipeline`. The npm city packs are unchanged: a page downloads only its city's generators.
  - **Attribution:** every source the build uses, now including `micro.dem`'s and the canopy map's where only street trees or `ground.lawn.wooded` read it.
  - **Feature rows:** the contract is Overture's schema (fields and vocabularies), so the build's consumers are unchanged; another source maps its classes to Overture's words. The row helpers (`path_class`, `rail_open_parts`, `rail_matches`) moved to sources/features.py.
- **Neutral:** cache keys hold only a source's kind until it has options, so every cache still hits. Nine worlds rebuilt from the same cache (Jerome; San Francisco's walking and flying levels; the walking levels of Paris, Kyoto, New York, Tokyo, Kowloon and the Southwest) have every chunk, object, route and attribution file byte for byte as before; the manifest differs only in the spec's digest, `generator.params.features` (was `overtureRelease`) and so `packageHash`.
- **Rejected alternatives:**
  - **One `pixelworld-cities` distribution:** keeps a split that buys nothing to install.
  - **Keeping the old keys beside `kind:`:** two styles of options; nothing is published yet to stay compatible with.
  - **A neutral feature schema of our own:** every consumer would change, for a mapping Overture already is (it is built from OpenStreetMap's).
- **Later:** stage hooks (`pixelworld.stages`); land cover in schemes other than ESA WorldCover's (a source maps to its codes today); one cache helper for every source (some import `CACHE_ROOT` by value, and canopy, lidar and Wikidata fetch without `http.get`'s retries).

### D-111: Multiplayer-friendly without netcode in the engine: world queries without a view, and entities that don't wait (accepted)
- **Why:** games on pixelworld should be able to go multiplayer, a dogfight over the Grand Canyon for one, but the engine draws one player's view and has no need of a network to do it. Each client draws its own view; other players are entities the game moves. What a game can't do on its own is give its server the same heights and occupancy its players' views read, or keep other players' constant pose changes from holding up `ready()`.
- **What:**
  - **`openWorld({ worldUrl, style, base?, renderScale?, objects? })`** (`@pixelworlds/engine`, `pixelworld/engine`): `{ world, query }`, the same `WorldInfo` and `WorldQuery` a view has, over the same package and style, with no canvas, GPU or page. `pick` throws. One function, `worldQuery`, builds the queries for both, so they can't drift apart. `loadWorld` no longer reads `location`, and resolves a relative URL only where there is a page; outside one, `worldUrl` is absolute or relative to `base`. Tested in Node on `southwest-fly` (the South Rim stands more than 500 m over Phantom Ranch; Hoover Dam occupies its cell).
  - **`EntitySpec.wait`** (default true): false for other players and effects. Their sprites don't make a frame incomplete, and their bakes (`Entities.unwaitedBakes`) are left out of the pending sprites `ready()` waits for, so they hold up neither `ready()`, the start of game time, nor a view replacing another.
- **Rejected alternatives:**
  - **Netcode in the engine:** transport, rooms, prediction and smoothing are a game's, and the engine would carry them for every single-player page.
  - **Lockstep on the deterministic controllers (D-015):** they are deterministic in one JavaScript engine, but `Math.sin`, `exp` and `atan2` may differ in their last bits between V8, SpiderMonkey and JavaScriptCore, so players on different browsers would drift apart. A server that owns the state, with each client predicting its own vehicle, tolerates that.
  - **`wait: false` by default:** a single-player game's own vehicle should be drawn in the first complete frame, as the rover and the example game's car and plane are.
- **Later:**
  - **Sprite bakes go to the workers ahead of every tile** (`BakeService.pump`). Planes rolling and looping need new poses all the time (16 headings × pitch and roll in 10° steps, per rotation), and several of them could starve the terrain ahead of a fast camera. Measure it with a sample game first; then cap sprite jobs to some of the workers, or bake a pose sheet per model once.
  - **Game time a game can set**, so fog, palette cycling and traffic agree with a server's clock.
  - **Traffic around several foci**, one per player, for a server to run when a game collides with traffic; today each player's view has its own.
  - **Edits as operations** a game can send, when building on the grid lands.
  - **A `@pixelworlds/multiplayer` package** for the sample games (a dogfight, a race) and for consumers: optional, and never imported by the engine. Built: D-112.

### D-112: `@pixelworlds/multiplayer`, relay-first, and the example game's dogfight over the Grand Canyon (accepted)
- **Why:** sample games (a dogfight now, a race next) and consumers need the same plumbing: rooms, a way to send state, and other players drawn smoothly. Netcode stays out of the engine (D-111), so it is a package of its own. Relay-first, because the open question is whether dogfighting in an isometric view is fun, not cheating, and a relay needs no game code on the server.
- **What:**
  - **The package** (`packages/multiplayer`, private until released):
    - `attachRelay(server, { path, maxPlayers, maxMessage, lag })` (`@pixelworlds/multiplayer/server`, Node, on `ws`): rooms by `?room=` on one path (`/relay`), ids from the relay, `welcome`, `join`, `leave`, `msg` (with `from`) and `full`. A message that isn't JSON is dropped; 16 KB at most; a heartbeat drops a dead connection within 10 s; an empty room goes. Other upgrades on the server (Vite's HMR) are left alone. `lag` holds every message back, to try smoothing under lag.
    - `joinRoom(url, room)`: `id`, `peers`, `send`, `onMessage`, `onJoin`, `onLeave`, `onClose`. It rejects when no relay answers (browsers fire error then close on a refused connection; Node's WebSocket only error) or the room is full.
    - `Smoothing`: poses stamped with the sender's clock, our clock less theirs taken at its smallest, drawn 0.1 s behind and in between; angles the short way round; a `jump` (a respawn) is not slid to. `RemotePlayers`: an entity per player with `wait: false`, moved each frame; `pose(id)` is where it was drawn, for aiming and hits.
  - **The dogfight** (`apps/game/src/dogfight.ts`, `?dogfight=<room>&name=`): the flying level over the inner canyon. Each window sends its plane 20 times a second. Space fires 11 rounds a second; a round aims itself at a plane within 0.2 rad of the nose, since depth is hard to judge in an isometric view. The shooter judges hits against the planes as it draws them; the one hit takes the damage (10 of 100), and a crash within 6 s of a hit counts for the shooter. Respawns land within 8 cells of the start. The others have shadows, and tags with their name, health, distance and height over or under yours, which point from the canvas edge to planes out of view. `Game.stay` keeps the map and the plane. The relay runs on the game's dev and preview servers.
  - **Checked:** Node tests of the relay (rooms kept apart, a full room, no relay, lag in order) and of the smoothing. Two headless Chromium players in one room each saw the other, the tag and the arrow; on the other's tail at 3 cells, one shot the other down in 1.3 s, and both boards agreed.
- **Rejected alternatives:**
  - **Vite's HMR channel as the transport:** no dependency, but it exists only on a dev server, and its API is for module updates.
  - **WebSocket framing written on `node:http`:** about a hundred lines of protocol code to save one small dependency.
  - **The server running the game first:** it needs the flight model on the server (it lives in the game) and prediction on the client. The shape for when cheating matters: a shared simulation stepped at a fixed rate, client prediction with corrections, and hits checked against where players were when the shot was fired.
- **Later:** publishing the package (a changeset, the release workflow and npm's trusted publisher); a mode where the server runs the game (`openWorld` for its collisions, D-111; built: D-113, and the dogfight moved to it); a race sample; sprite bake priority measured with several planes (D-111); a livery per player (the plane's seed doesn't change its colours much).

### D-113: Rooms the server runs: the dogfight's server owns the planes, and each page predicts its own (accepted)
- **Why:** in a relayed room (D-112) each page flies its own plane and judges its own hits, so any page can cheat, and two pages can disagree. A server that runs the game decides for everyone. The pieces were there: `openWorld` gives a server the players' world (D-111), and the flight model is pure functions that already ran in Node.
- **What:**
  - **The package:** `attachRelay(server, { host })` runs a game per room (`RoomHost`: `join`, `message`, `leave`, `close`; it answers with `send` and `broadcast`, `from` `SERVER`). `lag` now holds messages back on each hop, the game's included. `Prediction`: inputs stepped at once and numbered; `reconcile(ack, restore)` puts the server's state back and steps the rest again. `Smoothing.time` says when what is drawn happened, on the sender's clock.
  - **The flight model shared:** `flightParams`, `levelOut`, `clearance`, `spawnPlane` and `drawnPitch` moved from the plane's controller into plane.ts, for the controller and the server alike.
  - **The dogfight's server** (`apps/game/src/dogfightServer.ts`, on `/dogfight?room=`):
    - Opens the flying level with `openWorld` from the dev server itself, the style pack from disk, and loads all of it (a few dozen chunks).
    - Ticks at a fixed 60 a second. Each page's numbered inputs queue and are stepped one a tick (a backlog caught up at most 8 at a time), so a page can't fly faster than the clock. Input values are clamped; out-of-order, malformed or excess inputs are dropped; names are cut to 20 characters.
    - Owns the guns (the cooldown counts the page's steps), rounds, hits, health, kills, crash credit and respawns.
    - Judges a round against where its shooter saw the targets: the page sends, with each input, when what it draws of the others happened on the server's clock (`seen`); the server rewinds the targets by that lag, at most 0.5 s, from a history of where each plane was.
    - Sends each page a snapshot 20 times a second: every plane (where it is drawn, its score, a respawn flag) and the page's own plane after its last input stepped. Fire, hit and down events go out as they happen.
  - **The page** (`dogfight.ts`): the plane's controller only draws; the page steps the plane a fixed tick at a time with its keys through `Prediction` and sends each input. Each snapshot puts the plane back and steps the inputs not yet stepped; a respawn moves the camera with it. The others are drawn 0.1 s behind; rounds are drawn at once for its own shots and from the server's events for the others'. The board shows how many inputs are ahead of the server and the last correction.
  - **Where it runs:** on the game's dev server, loaded through Vite's module runner once the server listens (the config loader leaves workspace packages to Node, which can't load their TypeScript). `DOGFIGHT_LAG=ms` holds messages back each way. `vite preview` has no dogfight server.
  - **Checked:** Node tests of hosted rooms and `Prediction`; of the dogfight server over the committed flying level (a page predicting with the same inputs ends exactly where the server says; one step a tick; untrusted inputs dropped or clamped; ten hits shoot a plane down, its shooter scores and it respawns flagged; a crash within 6 s of a hit counts for the shooter). Two headless Chromium pages on the dev server: steering through rolls and pulls, the largest correction was 0.0000 cells with up to five inputs ahead of the server; the other page drew every round fired; crashes respawned.
- **Rejected alternatives:**
  - **The relayed dogfight beside it:** two dogfights to keep. The relay stays in the package for games that want one.
  - **Lockstep or rollback:** the model's float maths may differ between browsers' engines in the last bits (D-111); a server with corrections absorbs that.
  - **The server loaded from the Vite config's own imports:** Node can't load the engine's TypeScript sources; a built server bundle would work, as a build step to keep.
- **Later:** a server for production (a bundle of `dogfightServer.ts` beside the built game; built: D-114, where these files moved to the package's demos); a mode that ensures the world around each plane for big levels; hits shown at the moment a page's round meets a target rather than at the server's word; a race sample.

### D-114: The game the server runs, made generic in the package; the dogfight a demo of it, with a production server and an image (accepted)
- **Why:** the multiplayer code sat in the example game, part generic and part the dogfight's: the fixed tick, input queues, acks and rewinding were written for planes. A race, or anyone else's game, would have copied them. And the dogfight ran only on a dev server.
- **What:**
  - **Generic, in `@pixelworlds/multiplayer`:**
    - `TickRoom<I>` (server): a hosted room's game at a fixed tick. Each player's numbered inputs queue and are stepped one a tick (a backlog caught up at most `maxBurst` at a time); out-of-order, excess and invalid inputs are dropped; names are sanitized; players wait until the game's world is ready; snapshots carry `t`, `ack` and the game's fields. A game writes `input`, `added`, `removed`, `step`, `update` and `snapshot`; `lagOf(seen, max)` turns what a page saw into a rewind.
    - `History` (server): where something was by server time, for lag compensation; cleared across a jump.
    - `serveStatic(root)` (server): a built game beside the rooms on one port. GET and HEAD only, nothing outside its directory (dot segments, encoded ones and null bytes refused), `index.html` for a directory, hashed `/assets/` cached for good, world package files as stored.
    - `TickClient<I>` (page): the fixed ticks in each frame's time, each input predicted, numbered and sent with `seen`, `after` for effects drawn at once; `reconcile` on each snapshot. `TickMessage` and `TickSnapshot` are the protocol.
  - **The dogfight, a demo** (`packages/multiplayer/src/demos/dogfight/`): `rules.ts`, `server.ts` (a `TickRoom`), `client.ts` (a `TickClient`), a page of its own (`main.ts`, `index.html`) on the example game, and `serve.ts`. It imports the game's plane, flight model and style packs from `@pixelworlds/game` (a dev dependency of the package; the demos aren't in what it ships). The example game has no multiplayer code again; its page's CSS moved to `src/game.css`, which the demo's page imports, and its style packs to `src/styles.ts`, which the demo's server reads (bundled, not from disk).
  - **Production:** `demo:dogfight:build` builds the page with only the world it flies (`southwest-fly`, 1.4 MB with the page) and the server as one Node file with everything in it (380 KB); `demo:dogfight:start` runs it on `PORT` (8080). It used 79 MB with a room open. A `Dockerfile` (built from the repository's root; a root `.dockerignore` keeps local files and the other worlds out) and a `fly.toml` (one machine, as rooms live in memory; it stops when idle).
  - **Checked:** Node tests of `TickRoom` (on a toy game), `History`, `TickClient` and `serveStatic` (traversal refused), and of the dogfight demo over the committed flying level. Two headless Chromium pages on the demo's dev server and on the built production server: both joined, steering never needed a correction (0.0000 cells, up to 7 inputs ahead), every round fired reached the other page.
- **Rejected alternatives:**
  - **The dogfight as a mode of the example game:** the game would carry multiplayer code and its server; a demo shows what the package does with the game as a dependency.
  - **The flight model moved into a package:** it is the game's, and a demo may depend on the game.
  - **The production server through Vite (`vite preview` with a plugin):** a dev tool in production; a built Node file has nothing to install.
  - **Every world package in the build:** 85 MB for a demo that flies one.
- **Later:** pre-baked start tiles (`tools/prebake.mjs`) in the build, so the first view doesn't bake on the page (a headless page joined in about 20 s, most of it baking on SwiftShader); compression of the page's scripts; deploying on push; the race demo.

### D-116: The game the server runs, made sturdier and open to other modes: a join flow, roles, commands, bots, shared snapshots, one server clock (accepted)
- **Why:** a review of the package against modes beyond the dogfight (a race against traffic, team modes with a match, bots, spectators, building, more players) found the seams mostly right, but four robustness gaps and missing hooks:
  - **A server stall lagged for good.** After a stall the server's clock falls behind; with exactly one input credited a tick, the backlog it left never drained (about 350 ms of input lag after a 600 ms stall, every player, for the rest of the match). Smoothing's smallest-gap clock never recovered either, and lag compensation switched itself off.
  - **A world that failed to open stranded the room:** its pages waited at "joining…" for good.
  - **Rooms were unbounded,** each with a clock at 120 Hz: any page could open as many as it liked.
  - **A connection was a player at once,** named "Pilot n" in generic code: a game couldn't refuse one, hold it, take a team or a role with it, or seat a spectator. Only inputs reached the game, so a team pick or a ready would have had to ride in a lossy per-tick input. Snapshots were built and serialized once per player. Bots would have had to fake sockets.
- **What:**
  - **Robustness:** inputs are credited 1.02 a tick (a backlog drains: 8 queued inputs in a few hundred ticks), and a player's queue keeps 15, dropping the oldest (its page's prediction corrects). A page's view of another clock is a `ServerClock`: the quickest message's gap over the last 10 s, so it comes back after a stall; one per hosted room. `TickRoom`'s `ready` failing closes the room with the reason, which reaches the page (`bye` → `onClose(why)`). `maxRooms` (64), told to the page that would open one more.
  - **The relay:** `HostedRoom.kick(id, why)` and `close(why)`; `broadcast` serializes once.
  - **`TickRoom<I, J, W>`:** a page joins by saying who it is (`hello` with the game's join data, checked by `parseJoin`); once the world is ready, `added` makes it a player or a spectator, or turns it away; nothing in the generic code names anyone. Commands (`cmd`) reach `command`, from players and spectators. Bots: `addBot(id, join, drive)`, `removeBot`. Snapshots: `shared()` once for every page (`world`), `snapshot(id)` for each player (`snap`). `ready` is typed: `world` holds the game's world.
  - **The page:** `TickClient` has `hello(join)`, `command(c)`, `seen()` and `clock`; `Smoothing` and `RemotePlayers` take the room's clock; a pose's `life` replaces the respawn flag (a new life is drawn there at once); `RemotePlayers.retain(ids)` drops the players a snapshot no longer has.
  - **The dogfight on it:** spectators (`&watch=1`: no plane, they follow one, Space for the next); a bot when a pilot is alone (the example game's `FlightPilot` after the nearest plane, firing when it is near the nose), gone when another pilot arrives; lives instead of the respawn flag; events (shots, hits, downs) carry their server time and play when the page shows that time of the others, so they no longer run ahead of the planes.
  - **Checked:** Node tests of the join flow, roles, refusals, commands, bots, the credit draining a backlog, the queue dropping its oldest, a failing world closing the room, kicks and closes with reasons, the room cap, `ServerClock` recovering after a stall, and the dogfight's bot and spectators over the committed flying level. On the built production server with three headless pages: alone, a pilot got the bot, which hunted it and fired; a second pilot sent the bot away; a watcher followed a plane with its own hidden; steering never needed a correction (0.0000 cells).
- **Rejected alternatives:**
  - **Join data and commands inside the per-tick input:** dropped with it when a queue overflows, and stepped a tick at a time.
  - **A server-side room clock driven by the page clocks:** the server's own ticks are the authority; pages only read them.
  - **Waiting for the race demo for the hooks:** the review's choice; the owner chose to do them now, with the dogfight exercising spectators and bots.
- **Later** (the review's, for the race demo): smoothing a prediction's corrections; `History` of more than a position (a heading, for oriented hits); a player's step count for race times; a step that waits while its area loads; a policy for a player whose inputs stop; resuming after a reconnect; traffic around several foci, fed by the server. And the watcher's HUD still shows its own idle plane.

### D-118: Multiplayer from a second review: errors kept to their room, a lobby and match phases, a backed-up connection, events and input numbers, diagnostics, limits, and netcode tests run whole (accepted)
- **Why:** a second review set the package beside a full-featured server-run browser game in production. The netcode's core matched (a fixed tick, numbered inputs, a credit of 1.02 a tick, rewinding to what the page saw, prediction and replay); what was missing was around it. The owner asked for the review's to-dos.
- **What** (package, unless it says the demo):
  - **A game's error stays in its room.** The relay calls a room's game inside a guard, and a `TickRoom` its own ticks: a throw logs, closes that room ("the game hit an error") and leaves the server and its other rooms running (`HostedRoom.fail`). A page that sent a message over the size made `ws` emit an error on a socket nothing listened to, which would have ended the process: sockets' errors now close the socket. `relay.close(why)` sends every page away with the reason (the demo's server says it on a deploy).
  - **Diagnostics:** `TickClient.ping` (an input to the snapshot that has it); `TickRoom.stats()` and log notes for a clock that fell behind or a slow tick; `RelayOptions.log` for rooms opening and closing, pages joining and leaving, errors (the demo's server logs a line each).
  - **Input numbers and events:** `step(id, input, seen, seq)` and `advance`'s `after(input, seq)` have the input's number; `TickRoom.emit(e, to?)` stamps events with the server time and `TickClient.due()` (an `EventQueue`) lets them out when the page shows that time. `TickClient` takes the room's messages itself and puts the newest snapshot in place once a frame, so a burst of snapshots after a stall replays its inputs once. The demo names each round by its shooter's input and ends the page's own where the server says it hit, so tracers no longer fly through planes.
  - **A backed-up connection:** per player, how much later than now its page shows the others (from `seen`), at its smallest lately (creeping up 0.5 ms an input); more than 0.25 s over that, it is behind and gets every 10th snapshot until it catches up. This holds behind a proxy that buffers for the server, as the demo's host does. `broadcast(data, except)`.
  - **The lobby:** `Relay.open` (4-character codes without look-alikes), `list` (public rooms, busiest first, with `RoomHost.info`), `quick`; empty rooms kept a while (`idle`); `lobbyOnly`; `lobbyHandler` for `GET/POST /api/rooms` and `POST /api/quick`; `listRooms`, `openRoom`, `quickRoom` for pages. The demo: quick play without `?room=`, the code in the address and an invite link on the board.
  - **Match phases:** `Phases<P>` on the server's clock. The demo: a countdown with the guns cold, play to 10 kills or 5 minutes, the result, the next match with the scores reset; empty rooms wait; quick play skips rooms with under a minute left.
  - **Limits:** messages of 4 KB at most, 90 a second per connection with a burst of 180 (more dropped; a page that keeps on is sent away); `cleanName` (letters, digits, spaces, `-_.'`; no control or direction characters), which the demo uses.
  - **Netcode tested whole:** `loopback` (`@pixelworlds/multiplayer/testing`): a room and its pages in memory on a made-up clock, messages copied through JSON as they are sent and delivered after each page's latency, and stalls. Tests on it: a page's prediction exact at 50 ms each way (a toy game, and the dogfight's real flight model rolling and pulling), ping 100 ms; after a 0.6 s stall the page's lag back to normal, where a credit of 1 and a long queue kept 15 inputs more for good; a backed-up page getting under a third of the snapshots. Writing it found that a copy taken at delivery let a snapshot carry later state than its `ack`; the loopback copies as it sends, as the wire does.
  - **The demo's tick** (`flyTick`): the flight model, then any −0 in the state made 0, on both sides, as JSON writes −0 as 0 and atan2 tells them apart. Insurance: no divergence was traced to it.
  - **Checked:** 663 tests in the workspace. Live on the built production server with two headless pages: quick play put both in one room with a code in the address; the lobby listed it with its phase; the match ran from the countdown into play; the bot left when the second pilot came; a page's own rounds were drawn at once and every one reached the other page; ping 12–28 ms; no correction; the server logged the room opening and each page's coming and going.
- **Later** (from the review): identity and progress kept by the browser, signed by the server (and so reconnecting into one's seat); bots with a skill (reaction time, aim drift) that give seats up to humans; binary messages and a roster sent when it changes; the cross-browser check on Firefox and WebKit; deploying on push.

### D-119: The race demo: cars round the Strip run by the server, bumps judged where each driver saw the others, race times in a car's own steps, bots that overtake, and no traffic (accepted)
- **Why:** the second demo of `@pixelworlds/multiplayer`, and the first on the ground: the owner asked for a race on the example game's car, to exercise the package in another mode and to settle what the reviews left for it (D-116, D-118 "Later"): corrections that don't snap, race times in a player's own steps, a policy for a player whose inputs stop, fairness in car-to-car contact (players are stepped in join order, and the game's "from behind" rule favoured whoever joined first), a `History` of more than a position, and what to do about traffic.
- **What:**
  - **The car shared** (the example game): `carParams(world)` and `carSize(world)` in car.ts, out of `CarController.attach`, as `flightParams` was for the plane (D-113); the controller and the race's server both use them. `CarController.drawOffset`: the car and the camera drawn off its state, for a correction easing out.
  - **The package** (additive; nothing else changed):
    - `Correction` (page): `moved(before, after)` on each reconcile, `offset` to draw at, `update(dt)` easing it out (half-life 0.1 s), drawn at once past `snap` (3 cells: a respawn, the grid), `reset()`, `size`.
    - `TickRoom.idleFor(id)`: seconds since a player's last input was stepped (0 for a bot).
    - `History` and `Place`: an optional `heading`, rewound the short way round as `Smoothing` turns. Recorded with each shared snapshot, a rewind falls between the samples a page draws between.
    - The scripts `demo:race`, `demo:race:build`, `demo:race:start`.
  - **The race** (`packages/multiplayer/src/demos/race/`, the dogfight's files for a car):
    - **The course:** the Southwest's driving level (100 m cells at the Strip), clockwise round the Strip, Sahara, Paradise Road and Tropicana, about 127 cells, planned on the routes through five waypoints by the game's `RoadGraph` with joins at 1.5 cells (its 2.5 crossed a building's corner at a divided junction). Eight checkpoints evenly round it, passed within 2.5 cells in order; the line on the straight between Tropicana and Flamingo, the grid behind it in single file, a tenth of a cell either side in turn (roads are a quarter cell wide, and cars side by side closed on the line and locked).
    - **A race,** with `Phases`: waiting (no driver), an 8 s countdown with the cars held on the grid, three laps (at most 300 s; 30 s more once the winner is in), 12 s of results (times, best laps), the next. A driver who arrives mid-race waits on the grid as a ghost; quick play skips racing rooms. R puts a car back at its last checkpoint (an input, so the page predicts it).
    - **Race times in a car's own steps:** the racer's state, which the page predicts, counts its steps from the start while it isn't held; a lap is the steps at the line. The page's clock is the server's to the tick, however late its inputs arrive, and a burst of inputs after a stall doesn't make a car quicker. Nothing in the package: the count has to be in the predicted state.
    - **Bumps judged where the driver saw the others:** before each step the server gives the car's world the other cars as traffic (`DriveWorld.obstacle`, oriented boxes), rewound to the input's `seen` through `History` (a bot: where they were as the tick began). Nobody is tested against where another moved this tick, so the order of steps doesn't matter (a test runs two cars nose to tail in both orders: the same). The page steps against the others where its `Smoothing` draws them, and each input keeps the cars it saw, so a replay bumps the same: with the bots around it, the page's prediction stayed exact through the loopback at 50 ms each way; with the server bumping into the others where they were now, the same run needed corrections of 0.86 cells. The game's rule stays: the car in front isn't slowed by one behind. Favouring the driver, as the dogfight favours the shooter: a car close behind another bumps where its driver sees it, a little further back than it is. Cars bump at half their drawn width (`raceCar`, both sides): at this level a car is drawn 0.31 cells wide on roads a quarter cell wide, and at its whole width no driver could get by another; nose to tail they bump at their whole length.
    - **Ghosts as they were:** which cars the others drive through (`CarNet.ghost`) is recorded at each shared snapshot on both sides (`Ghosts`), and a bump is judged with the flags as of the time the driver saw. Judged with the flags of now, a bot turning ghost to pass broke the page's prediction (0.84 cells in the loopback test).
    - **A page gone quiet:** stalled after 0.5 s (`idleFor`); the others drive through it, as through a finished car or one waiting for the next race, and its tag says so. Its car stands where it was until its inputs come back.
    - **Corrections** drawn with `Correction` (the car and the camera ease); what the page can't predict remains: the release at the start and the freeze at the end (the server's word), and another car's reset (`History` is cleared across it, while `Smoothing` holds its old life a moment).
    - **Bots** make up the field to four between races (none without a driver), on the game's `CarPilot` along the course's line, at 100, 80 and 62 % of the pace (the share of each road's top speed, and the pace the pilot corners and brakes at: the course is all corners, and top speed alone made a few tenths a lap), on the grid behind the drivers, slowest first. Stuck 1.5 s, back to their last checkpoint; past the flag, they stop.
    - **Overtaking:** a bot held up behind a slower car (within 1.3 cells, in its way) passes it as a ghost: nothing bumps it and it bumps nothing, until it is clear ahead of that car (0.9 cells) or gives up (1.5 cells behind, or 4 s), and not while it would be inside another car. So that it reads as a pass, its path eases 0.15 cells to the side away from the other where the course runs straight (one-cell jogs allowed) and that side has room (mapped once round the course, 0.3 cells off the line), and eases back. A bot that isn't passing keeps back from a slower car rather than ram it. In Node, alone with the bots, the quickest came through from the back to win by 0.9 s and 9 s; a driver at 3 cells/s was passed by all three; none of them stuck. Passing sideways was tried first and given up: the pilot's tracking (0.1–0.15 cells) is about the road's width, so a lane far enough out to clear a car on the line ran onto the buildings at the jogs and round the corners.
    - **No traffic:** the page's game has none (`traffic: { enabled: false }`), and the server has none to bump.
    - **The page:** gates at the checkpoints, the line chequered, the next gate taller in gold; "Up to P2" and "Down to P3" as its place changes; the countdown's numbers as the page's own inputs will reach the server, "Go!" when its car is let go; tags with place and name; the board with place, lap, time, best lap, the standings or the results, ping, inputs ahead and the last correction; zoom 2.
  - **Production:** as the dogfight's: `demo:race:build` builds the page with only `southwest` (7.1 MB with the page) and the server as one Node file (430 KB); `demo:race:start` runs it on `PORT`; a `Dockerfile`.
  - **Checked:** 677 tests in the workspace. Node tests of the course, the grid, lap counting and timing (a short cut doesn't count; each lap's steps), a reset, a bump and the rule from behind, the race's phases with bots making up the field and the results, a bot's lap of the Strip, bumps the same in either order of joining, a stalled car driven through, clamped inputs and a late driver held for the next race, bots passing a slow driver as ghosts and solid again once clear, and prediction exact through the loopback with bumps and bots passing; of `idleFor`, `History`'s heading and `Correction`; and `carParams` against the controller's. Live on the built production server with two headless Chromium pages and a watcher (the pages' cars on the course's line through the car controller's path pilot, their inputs through the `TickClient`): quick play put the first in a room and the code in the address; the second joined by the code, the watcher too, and saw all four cars; a full race (the pages finished in 1:10.9 and 1:11.8), the results and the next race; no correction during the race (the most, 0.001 cells, as a car was let go at the start), up to 9 inputs ahead, ping 9–32 ms, at 12–45 frames a second on SwiftShader. In an earlier run, with a third page baking and the drivers' pages down to 10 frames a second, the largest correction was 0.35 cells, drawn easing out. With overtaking, and the second page held to 3.5 cells/s: the bots passed both pages (their passing flags up about 8 s in all), and neither page needed a correction (the most drawn, 0.004 cells). On the dev server: joining, the countdown and the race, and a driver arriving mid-race held for the next.
- **Rejected alternatives:**
  - **Traffic as a ghost** (drawn, bumping nothing): cars driving through cars reads as a bug, and each page would still run its own simulation. **Traffic from the server:** traffic around several foci (D-111 "Later") and sending it, for a demo that's about the cars racing. Off is the lightest, and a race closes its streets.
  - **Bumps against where the others are now** (their positions as the tick began): as fair, but no page can know them, so every bump is corrected (0.86 cells in the loopback test). **In the order cars are stepped:** what the review found unfair.
  - **No car-to-car contact:** the simplest, but contact is part of a race, and the review asked for it to be fair.
  - **Holding the last input of a player whose inputs stopped:** the server would step inputs its page never predicted (a correction when it comes back) and drive a car blind.
  - **Race times in server time:** a player's inputs arrive late and in bursts; its own steps are what it drove.
  - **Two abreast on the grid:** side by side, they close on the line and lock.
  - **Overtaking in lanes beside the line, the cars solid:** a lane far enough out to clear a car on the line (0.34 cells at their drawn width, 0.2 at half) runs onto the buildings at the jogs and round the corners once the pilot's tracking error is added; offset lanes only on straights and where mapped open left too few chances, and a pass outlasted them, as the bots differ by a few tenths a lap.
- **Later:** bots that overtake on the racing line into a corner rather than alongside; `History` across another car's reset, for prediction exact there too; more courses (San Francisco); quick play holding a room for a page still loading (seen live: the room closed meanwhile and the page reopened it by name, unlisted); the watcher's HUD still shows its own idle car (D-116's); pre-baked start tiles (the page asks for `start-tiles.bin` and gets a 404, as the dogfight's does).

### D-120: Rooms the server runs on worlds too big to load whole, with more players: a chunk budget, loading around each player, a step that waits, and interest (accepted)
- **Why:** the reviews of D-116 and D-118 left big levels and more players for later. Each gap, checked first:
  - **The dogfight's server loaded its whole level:** fine for `southwest-fly` (24 chunks, 12 MB of decoded terrain), not for the driving levels or the cities. The southwest whole is 228 chunks and 112 MB (its process went from 123 to 338 MB RSS).
  - **`ChunkStore` never let a chunk go, and placed objects load whole:** nyc's 282,000 objects take about 116 MB of heap and sf's 251,000 about 110 MB, more than their terrain (98 and 110 chunks).
  - **`ensure` is async and a tick sync:** nothing held a player whose ground wasn't loaded. A plane over terrain not loaded read no ground and flew on.
  - **Every page got every player in each shared snapshot:** 82 KB/s a page with 40 players.
- **What:**
  - **Engine and runtime** (nothing changes unless asked):
    - **A chunk budget:** `ChunkStore.maxBytes`. Past it, after each load, the least recently used chunks go (a read, an `ensure` or a check is a use), but never one an `ensureRect` holds: from its call until a task after it resolves, so the reads that follow its await (a tile's slice, a step) always find their chunks. A chunk is about 0.5 MB at 64 cells with micro-terrain at k 4: 94 bytes a cell (22 of layers, 72 of road, path and rail pieces) and the micro-corners.
    - **`chunkBudgetMB`** on `openWorld` and `createWorldView` (default unlimited). **`query.isLoaded(x0, y0, x1, y1)`**: whether the terrain under a rectangle is loaded now; a chunk the package lacks counts as loaded, as there is nothing to wait for. **`OpenedWorld.stats()`** and `ViewStats`: `chunksLoaded`, `chunksTotal`, `chunksMB`, `chunksEvicted`. The viewer takes `?chunkBudget=` (MB).
  - **The package** (additive):
    - **A step that waits:** `step` may return false. Then it hasn't happened: the input stays first in its queue, spends no credit and isn't acknowledged. The credit saved while waiting (at most `maxBurst`) steps a burst once it can; the rest of the backlog drains as after a stall (D-116).
    - **Interest:** `groupOf(id)` and `shared(group?)`. With groups, each group's shared snapshot is built and serialized once (`broadcast` with the other pages excepted), and pages in no group (a watcher, a page not yet admitted) get the whole one; without, one broadcast as before. `emit(e, to?)` also takes a list of ids.
    - **`Interest(cell, reach)`** (server): things filed in squares; a page's group is its square, and it gets what is within `reach` squares: everything within `cell × reach` cells, some up to `cell × (reach + 1)`. `place`, `remove`, `groupOf`, `near(group)`, `around(x, y)` for events.
    - **`Areas(query, { radius, ahead, reach })`** (server): `track` each player each tick: a square of `radius` around it and around where it will be `ahead` seconds on, snapped to 8 cells, asked for once while it loads. While it is loaded, `track` is a check that marks it used, so a budget lets go of the areas no one is near first. `ready(x, y)` for the step.
    - **`RemotePlayers.retain` and `Smoothing`, unchanged:** a player that leaves the snapshot goes with its samples; one that comes back is drawn where it is at once (its first sample held until time reaches it), not slid from where it left. Now tested.
  - **The dogfight:** opens the start's area and the ground under its landmarks (for the ceiling over their tops, each read just after its own load), within 64 MB. Then `Areas` keeps all a respawn reads around the start in use (32 cells: the spread, and the clearance a spawn looks for ahead and around), and loads around each plane (16 cells, and 2 s ahead); a plane over ground not yet loaded waits. At open: 8 of `southwest-fly`'s 24 chunks (3.9 MB); on the southwest, 9 of 228 (4.4 MB). It doesn't use interest: its level is small, and its board lists every pilot.
  - **Measured** (`packages/multiplayer/test/bigworld.test.ts`, `BIGWORLD_LOG=1 BIGWORLD_SECONDS=20`): 32 bots and 8 pages (`loopback`, 50 ms each way) as rovers on the southwest's ground at 6 cells/s, on a grid across all of it, a second to start and then 20 s measured; memory with `--expose-gc`, over what the process held before the world opened, each run alone; ranges over several runs.

    | | Before: the world whole, everyone to every page | After: `Areas` (8 cells, 2 s ahead) within 40 MB, `Interest(128)` |
    |---|---|---|
    | Terrain held | 228 chunks, 112 MB | 81 chunks, 40 MB (156 to 171 let go, and some loaded again, as rovers moved) |
    | Server memory, heap + buffers | 26 + 113 MB | 8 to 10 + 41 MB |
    | A tick: mean, p99 | 0.035 to 0.043, 0.10 to 0.20 ms | 0.058 to 0.088, 0.20 to 0.48 ms |
    | A page gets | 82 KB/s, 39 others | 12 KB/s, at most 6 others |
    | Steps that waited for their ground | 0 | about 2,500 in the first second (every rover on ground nothing had loaded, for about a second); then 50 to 230 of 48,000 |

    A tick costs about twice as much (the checks, a snapshot per group), still under 3% of the 16.7 ms it has at its p99; the longest were 0.5 ms before and 1.1 ms after, but for one run's 43 ms (a garbage collection, or the code warming up). The shared snapshot is serialized once per group: 8 at most here, where per page it would be 8 as well, but with 200 pages in 30 squares it would be 30.
  - **A view** (the viewer on the southwest at 2×, headless Chromium, its rover set down at eight places across the region in turn, each baked): unbounded it held 81 chunks (40 MB) at the end and grew at every place; with `?chunkBudget=24` it held 48 (23.7 MB), let 33 go, and baked the same 88 tiles. The smoke renders matched their goldens with the viewer's budget forced to 4 MB.
  - **Checked:** tests of the store's budget (least recently used first, the hold across an await, overlapping `ensureRect`s with loads landing in separate tasks, a chunk the package lacks), a step that waits (the queue, the credit, the ack), interest (each group once, the whole to pages in none, events to those near, one broadcast as before without groups), `Areas` (asked once while loading, ahead, again after a failure), `RemotePlayers` out of range and back, the dogfight waiting over ground not loaded and flying on, the dogfight under a 5 MB budget (planes sent to the four corners load and let go of theirs; the start's area stays), and the big-world run (every rover waits at first, chunks go, nobody stays stuck). A review of the change found the start's area kept only 16 cells around it, where a respawn reads 32 (a ridge in a chunk let go would be ignored), and the landmarks' tops read after all their loads; the 5 MB test failed on the first and both are fixed. The dogfight's tests flaked before this (10 of 25 runs failed on the base commit): planes respawn at `Math.random()` places, and some put a test's plane over terrain that doesn't do what the test expects. They now seed it. The workspace's tests and the e2e smoke tests pass.
- **API changed** (for the branches beside this one):
  - `@pixelworlds/engine`: `WorldQuery.isLoaded` (a new member: fakes add it); `OpenWorldOptions.chunkBudgetMB`, `WorldViewOptions.chunkBudgetMB`; `OpenedWorld.stats()`, `WorldStats`; `ViewStats.chunksMB`, `ViewStats.chunksEvicted`.
  - `@pixelworlds/runtime`: `ChunkStore.maxBytes`, `loadedBytes`, `evicted`; `isLoadedRect` marks chunks used and counts ones the package lacks as loaded. `QuerySource.isLoadedRect` (a new member).
  - `@pixelworlds/multiplayer`: `TickRoom.step` returns `void | boolean`; `TickRoom.groupOf(id)`; `TickRoom.shared(group?)`; `TickRoom.emit(e, to?: string | readonly string[])`; `Areas`, `AreasOptions`, `AreaWorld`, `Interest` (`/server`). The demo: `openDogfightWorld(url, { chunkBudgetMB })`, `DogfightWorld.opened`, `attachDogfight(server, { chunkBudgetMB })`.
- **Rejected alternatives:**
  - **`Areas` releasing the chunks no one is near itself:** a second way to let go beside the budget, over a store the objects' heights and anything else also read. One budget serves views and servers, and lets go of the same areas first.
  - **Each page's shared snapshot filtered to a radius around it:** serialized per page, which interest is there to avoid. Squares make one group per square, at the cost of seeing up to `cell × (reach + 1)` on one side.
  - **`HostedRoom.sendTo(ids, data)`:** a cleaner multicast, but a change to the interface the relay, the loopback and the parallel branches implement; `broadcast` with the rest excepted is serialized once too.
- **Later:** a timeout on chunk fetches: one that never answers holds its player for good (and its chunks against the budget); placed objects by chunk in the format, so they load and go with the terrain (the cities' 110 MB of heap); interest with some hysteresis, so a page on a square's edge doesn't see players far off come and go; a roster (names, scores) sent when it changes, so a board can list everyone while positions go by interest; a budget for the bake workers' copies of chunks; traffic around several foci (D-111).

### D-121: Fogfront, a real-time strategy basis run by commands: the Presidio's cells for paths and building places, the fog of war per side, and commands acknowledged in snapshots (accepted)
- **Why:** the owner wants game modes that exercise the engine and `@pixelworlds/multiplayer`, each on its own branch; this one is the basis of a base-building strategy game (gather, build, train, move and fight in groups). Such a game is driven by orders, not by a vehicle's input every tick, so it tests whether `TickRoom` fits a game with no inputs, many units and a fog of war, and reads the world's cells (slope, water, objects, roads) for paths and building places rather than for one vehicle's collisions.
- **What:**
  - **The game** (`packages/multiplayer/src/demos/rts/`, original in name, sides, rules and look): two guilds, the Kiln (red) and the Tide (blue), race for glim on the Presidio, on San Francisco's driving level at 25 m a cell (132×82 cells: the Kiln on Baker Beach, the Tide on Crissy Field, three seeps by each hearth, two in clearings between). Runners carry glim from seeps to their hearth; the hearth trains runners; a lodge (placed, 15 s to rise) trains wardens, who fight; a side whose hearth falls, or whose player leaves, loses. Orders: move, fight-move, attack, gather, stop; idle wardens take on what comes into sight.
  - **The map from the world's queries** (`buildGrid`): `heightAt`, `surfaceAt` (water), `roadAt` and `objectsAt` at each cell's middle, read once (110 ms) by the server through `openWorld` and by the page through its view. Water and the level's buildings are walls; a step to a neighbour climbs at most 1.5 height steps (the bluffs); roads are 1.5× quicker and woods (trees) 0.6× and unbuildable. Trees as walls cut the beach off from the rest of the Presidio, so they slow instead.
