Paths are A* on the cells (eight ways, no corner cutting, the nearest reachable cell for a goal that can't be reached), straightened where a straight line is clear and no slower; at most 16 are found a tick (an A* over the map is 1–2 ms, so a page re-sending orders for many units can't stall the server), and a unit that reaches the end of its path without getting to its seep or its foe lets the order go. A building goes up on dry, level ground (1 step across it) with no object, woods, building, unit or seep on it, within 12 cells of one's own; the page checks the same before it sends (a ghost coloured by the answer), the server again. Buildings are entities and the match's own grid; the engine is unchanged (editing the grid is D-111's "Later").
  - **The server:** a `TickRoom` at 20 ticks a second, 10 snapshots. Orders are commands (`TickClient.command`), checked for shape and sense, refused with a reason (an event to that page); nothing is a per-tick input (`input` gives null). Each player's snapshot (`snapshot(id)`) is its side's view, built once a tick: its own units with where they go, the other's within sight of its units and buildings, the hearths from the start and other buildings once seen, positions to 0.01 cells. The shared snapshot has the match (`Phases`: waiting, playing, ended) and who plays. A page alone gets the bot after 20 s, or at once (`vs: "bot"`); a third page is turned away with a reason. Events (blows, deaths) go to the pages whose side sees where they happened. The lobby's quick play picks a room waiting for a second side.
  - **The bot** (`bot.ts`): a side the server plays (`addBot`), deciding once a second from what its side sees, through the same orders: six runners, a lodge (a second when glim piles up, each with open ground round it), wardens, every warden at foes near its hearth, and its idle wardens at the other hearth five at a time, then three.
  - **The page:** its own view of the level with a small camera controller (keys and a middle drag pan over the play area, Q/E turn, the wheel zooms). Units, buildings and seeps are entities built from boxes in the style pack's ramps (the sides' colours, glim's gold), `wait: false`; units are drawn 0.2 s behind on the server's clock with `Smoothing` (not `RemotePlayers`: a runner's model changes when it carries glim), with a silhouette in their side's colour where woods, the level's buildings or a hearth hide them. A click selects the unit drawn under it (or, with `query.pick`, a building); a drag selects one's units in the box; a right click attacks what is there, sends runners to a seep, or moves (`query.pick` for the ground). An order shows at once (a marker, and lines from the selected units to where they go) and holds until the snapshot that acknowledges it. The HUD: glim, the selection, the match, ping, an invite link, and buttons to train and build.
  - **The package, what a command-driven game needed** (additive):
    - **Commands acknowledged:** `TickMessage`'s `cmd` has an optional number `n`; `TickClient.command(c)` sends one and returns it (it returned nothing); `TickRoom` keeps each player's last, and its snapshots say it (`TickSnapshot.cmd`, absent until a page numbers one; in a binary snapshot, D-123, a number after `ack`, 0 for none). Without it a page drawing its own order at once would see the snapshots before the order put the old one back.
    - **A page with no vehicle:** `TickClient.sync()` puts the newest snapshot in place without stepping or sending anything (what `advance` does first, which now calls it); `ping` also comes from a command to the snapshot that acknowledges it.
    - **Fits as it is:** commands as the main channel (reliable, once, in order); bots whose `drive` gives a list of orders, applied in `step`; per-side views through `snapshot(id)`; `emit(e, to)` for refusals and for events only some may see; `Phases`; the lobby's `fits`; `loopback` (its `ready` resolves on a later turn, so a test awaits one before running, as the dogfight's does).
    - **The engine** (additive, a changeset): `EntitySpec.silhouetteRamp`, a ramp id whose lightest shade an entity's silhouette is drawn in (the compositor sets the colour per sprite, `SpriteInstance.silhouetteIndex`); without it, the style's one silhouette colour, which can't tell one side's units from the other's.
    - The demo's dev dependency `@pixelworlds/city-sf` (the page draws the level's buildings), and `demo:rts`, `demo:rts:build`, `demo:rts:start`.
- **Rejected alternatives:**
  - **Orders as per-tick inputs:** dropped when a queue overflows, stepped a tick at a time, and 20 empty inputs a second a page.
  - **Lockstep (only orders on the wire, every page running the match):** the maths may differ between browsers' engines (D-111), and every page would hold every unit, so the fog of war would be a page's to keep.
  - **Snapshots per team in the package now:** a side has one player in this basis, so `snapshot(id)` is a side's view; a team mode is the time for it (below).
  - **Woods as walls:** the Presidio's woods split the map into pockets with no way between the hearths.
  - **Buildings in the world's grid:** an engine feature for later (D-111); entities and the match's own grid do for a basis.
- **Later:**
  - **The package:** snapshots per team, serialized once for its players, and for spectators (a spectator gets only the shared snapshot, so a game with a fog of war can't seat one: Fogfront has none); sending what changed (a snapshot carries every unit a side sees each time: about 87 bytes a unit as JSON, 2.7 KB for 31, so 40 a side is about 35 KB a second a page at 10 snapshots; quantized binary or deltas past a few hundred); judging a connection backed up from commands too (it is judged from inputs' `seen`, so a page that only orders is never "behind"); `RemotePlayers` whose model can change.
  - **The game:** a refused building place says what stands there even where the fog hides it ("units stand there"); a fog drawn over what a side doesn't see; a minimap; control groups and rally points; more units and buildings; a building's ground written into the world's grid (D-111); the server reads the map on the first room's opening and its clock falls 120–200 ms behind once; sight and separation compare every pair (fine to a few hundred units).
- **Checked:** Node tests of paths (round a wall through its gap and round water, roads taken and woods gone round when quicker, no step over the climb, the nearest cell for an unreachable goal), placing (each reason, refused with no glim, a building blocking paths), gathering, training, a lodge rising, fights (a warden kills a runner in five blows, takes on the first foe in sight on a fight-move), a hearth falling, the fog of war, units on one spot stepping apart, runners sent to a walled-off seep letting it go, at most 16 paths a tick for 30-odd units ordered at once, commands' shapes; over the committed level, a straight-line-clear path from hearth to hearth; through the loopback, two pages taking a side each, orders acknowledged by number (ping 100–300 ms at 50 ms each way), another page's and bad orders refused to that page alone, a third page turned away, a page leaving losing; and the bot taking an idle page's hearth (139 s of play; a tick 0.3 ms). A toy room run by commands alone: numbered, acknowledged, `sync`, ping. An entity's silhouette in its own ramp's lightest shade; live, runners walked behind their hearth showed through it in the Kiln's colour. A review of the change found a page keeping a past match's seeps (their ids change with each match, so a right click on one was refused), a closing reason the board wrote over, units on one spot that never parted, and orders that could hold a unit for good or cost a path each a tick: fixed. 685 tests in the workspace. Live on the built production server (page 271 KB of script with the `sf` level beside it, server 405 KB) with two headless Chromium pages on SwiftShader: quick play and the code put them in one room in 2 s, each drew its side, neither saw the other's runners; a drag selected three runners, a right click's order showed at once and was acknowledged in 250–510 ms, a click selected one unit; a lodge placed with the mouse rose and trained a warden; a refused build said why; the warden walked out of the other page's fog in about 45 s and struck its hearth; ping 9–99 ms, 8–55 frames a second, no page errors.

### D-123: Big worlds made lighter: lean chunks, objects by chunk, coarse heights, compact binary snapshots, chunks by number, and a server reading its world from disk (accepted)
- **Why:** D-120 made big worlds work. The owner then asked for the twelve ways to make it cheaper that a brainstorm over its numbers turned up, all of them, in order.
- **What:**
  1. **Lean chunks:** `openWorld({ dropLayers: DRAW_ONLY_LAYERS })` (and `decodeChunk(buf, { drop })`, `ChunkStore.dropLayers`) leaves out the layers only drawing reads: the road, path and rail pieces (72 of a cell's 94 bytes), geology and rails. They read as empty: one zero-filled array, shared. No query reads them. A chunk with micro-terrain goes from about 0.5 MB to about 0.2 MB. The dogfight's server drops them.
  2. **Placed objects by chunk:** `pixelworld object-chunks <package>` (pipeline `object_chunks.py`) writes `objects/<x>_<y>.json.gz` beside the whole file, plus `objects/landmarks.json.gz` and the manifest's `objectChunks` (docs/world-format.md).
     - **What a chunk's file holds:** every instance without a landmark id whose bounds touch that chunk, so a point's chunk alone says what stands there. The landmarks have their own file.
     - **How `openWorld` uses them:** it loads each chunk's objects with its terrain and lets them go together. It indexes each chunk's on its first query (`ChunkedObjects`) and keeps the landmarks whole.
     - **Views are unchanged:** their bake needs the whole file.
     - **Checked:** at every object's anchor and inside every footprint corner of `southwest-fly`, the answers equal the whole file's.
     - **nyc around midtown (16 of 98 chunks):** 25 MB of heap more than before it opened, against 169 MB with the whole file. On a copy; the nyc fixture doesn't carry them.
  3. **Coarse heights:** `pixelworld coarse-heights <package>` writes `coarse.i16.gz`. For each block of 8 × 8 cells it holds the lowest drawn ground and the highest drawn top (ground or water), in 1/16 step.
     - **The query:** `query.coarseHeightAt(x, y)` answers whether or not the chunks are loaded.
     - **The dogfight:** a plane more than `resetAgl` clear of its block's top, under it and under its nose, flies on over ground still loading instead of waiting. Towers there are known a moment later, when the ground loads.
     - **Checked:** across `southwest-fly`, 30,000 points' ground and water lie between their block's two heights (TypeScript reading the Python writer's file).
     - **Size:** the southwest's file is 27 KB.
  4. **Compact binary:** a `Schema` says how each field goes, with `Writer` and `Reader` underneath:
     - `fixed(step)`: a position to a 64th of a cell is two or three bytes.
     - `angle(steps)`: read back in [−π, π).
     - `int`, `bool`, `str`.
     - `exact`: eight bytes.

     A hosted game's bytes go as binary frames (the relay, `joinRoom`, the loopback). `TickRoom.snapSchema` sends a player's own snapshot in binary, whole and exact, so its prediction still lands exactly where the server says (`readSnap`).
  5. **Keyframes then diffs:** with `TickRoom.replicator` (a `Replicator`), each group of pages gets one binary message a snapshot. Its `Replica` reads it on the page, through `TickClient`'s `schema`.
     - **Diffs:** a page that has had every message of its group since it joined gets only the fields that changed. WebSocket is reliable and ordered, so no acks are needed.
     - **Keyframes:** a page new to its group, or back from being skipped as backed up, gets a keyframe.
     - **Taken from webhawk:** compact binary for every snapshot, and the roster apart. Webhawk sends whole snapshots; the diffs are this decision's.
  6. **Nearer more often:** a group's `view` has a near ring, sent every snapshot, and a far one, sent every `farEvery`th (4). `Interest.near(group, reach)` gives the rings.
  7. **A roster when it changes:** `TickRoom.roster()` (names, teams, scores) goes to every page when its JSON changes, and to each page as it connects (`TickClient.onRoster`). The dogfight's board takes names and scores from it, and its planes go by replication.
  8. **Each state written once:** each thing is encoded at most twice a snapshot, whole and as a diff, whatever the number of groups and pages. A group's message is those pieces put together. A test spies on the schema to check.
  9. **Chunks by number:** the store keeps chunks in a flat array by `cy × cols + cx`, and the hottest read (`surfaceHeightAt`) makes no object. 2 million height reads on the southwest went from 199 to about 140 ms, and 200,000 area checks from 13 to 8 ms.
  10. **Lighter bookkeeping:**
      - `HostedRoom.sendTo(ids, data)` is optional: the relay and the loopback have it, and `TickRoom` falls back to a broadcast with the rest excepted.
      - `Areas.track` compares the area's corners rather than building a key.
      - The budget finds the least recently used chunk by a scan, not a sort.
      - A throttle on `track` was tried and dropped. It let the dogfight's start area go under a tight budget, and stopped a failed load being asked for again.
  11. **A world from disk:** `openWorld({ fetch })` replaces `fetch` for every file of the package. `fileFetch(root)` (`/server`) reads them from disk and nothing outside `root`. The dogfight's dev server and production bundle open their world that way instead of over HTTP from themselves. The built server opened `southwest-fly` from `dist/client` and replicated to a Node client in binary.
  12. **Interest:**
      - **`slack`:** a thing keeps its square until it is that far past the edge. On the big-world run, 16 cells cut the keyframes from pages changing square from 13 to 4.
      - **`diamond`:** the shape of an isometric view, everything within `cell × reach` counted across plus down. With 32-cell squares at reach 4, each page got at most 8 others instead of 11, and 3.2 KB/s instead of 3.3. 128-cell squares stay the default, since finer ones change group more often (30 keyframes).
- **Checked:**
  - **Tests:** 690 in the workspace, the pipeline's new ones, and the e2e smoke tests.
  - **Live:** the dogfight in headless Chromium, and its production bundle with a Node client.
  - **A review:** it ran the replication through 15,000 random runs and found six bugs, now fixed and tested:
    - A keyframe could list a thing as gone that it also had, back under a new number while the page was skipped. The dogfight's bot vanished for a few snapshots.
    - The diamond's slack costs `4 × slack` counted across plus down, not `2 ×` (the docs).
    - A room dropped what came before its page had a handler. Now it holds it, so a first keyframe isn't lost.
    - An infinite number would never have ended as a whole one on the wire.
    - `fileFetch` refused everything from a relative root.
    - A game's other binary messages now reach `onMessage`.
- **Measured** (`test/bigworld.test.ts`, as in D-120: 32 bots and 8 pages over the whole southwest, 20 s; memory alone in its process):

  | | D-120's before | D-120 | D-123 |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | Terrain held | 228 chunks, 112 MB | 81 chunks, 40 MB | 99 chunks, 19.8 MB (lean, a 20 MB budget) |
  | Server memory, heap + buffers | 26 + 113 MB | 8 to 10 + 41 MB | 6 + 22 MB |
  | A tick: mean, p99 | 0.033 to 0.043, 0.10 to 0.20 ms | 0.048 to 0.051, 0.19 to 0.22 ms | 0.050 to 0.054, 0.20 to 0.23 ms |
  | A page gets | 82 KB/s, 39 others | 12.3 KB/s, at most 7 others | 3.6 KB/s, at most 13 others (a far ring) |
  | Steps held for their ground | 0 | about 2,500 at the start, about 200 after | none: about 2,600 steps on coarse ground instead |

  The dogfight, per page: with 8 pilots, 38.4 KB/s of JSON before and 5.6 KB/s now (planes 2.8 KB/s, own snapshot 2.3 KB/s); with 2 pilots, 17.4 and 4.3 KB/s. Two headless Chromium pages on its dev server saw each other by name, with corrections of 0.0000 cells and a ping of 9 to 14 ms.
- **API changed:**
  - `@pixelworlds/world-format`:
    - `DRAW_ONLY_LAYERS`, `DroppableLayer`, `decodeChunk(buf, { drop })`.
    - `WorldFetch`, and a `fetcher` argument to `fetchWorldFile`, `fetchWorldJson` and `GeoGrid.load`.
    - The manifest's `objectChunks` and `coarse`.
  - `@pixelworlds/runtime`:
    - `ChunkStore.dropLayers`, `objectChunks`, `worldFetch` and `objectsIn`.
    - `chunkFetcher`, `loadObjectLandmarks`, `loadCoarse`, `CoarseHeights`.
    - A `fetcher` argument to `loadManifest` and `loadObjects`.
  - `@pixelworlds/engine`:
    - `OpenWorldOptions.dropLayers` and `fetch`; `DRAW_ONLY_LAYERS` re-exported.
    - `WorldQuery.coarseHeightAt`, a new member: fakes add it.
  - `@pixelworlds/multiplayer`:
    - The codec: `Schema`, `Writer`, `Reader`, and the fields `fixed`, `angle`, `int`, `bool`, `str` and `exact`.
    - Replication: `Replicator`, `Replica`, `ReplicaUpdate`, `WORLD_BYTE`, `SNAP_BYTE`, `readSnap`.
    - `TickRoom`: `replicator`, `snapSchema`, `roster()`.
    - `TickClient`: a fifth type parameter `R`, and `schema`, `snapSchema`, `onRoster`, `replica`. `TickRoster`.
    - The server side: `HostedRoom.sendTo?`; `HostedRoom.send` and `broadcast` take bytes. `Interest(cell, reach, { slack, diamond })`, `Interest.near(group, reach?)`, `fileFetch`.
    - The dogfight: `PLANE_WIRE`, `PLANE_STATE`, `PlaneWire`, `DogfightRoster`, `planesOf`, `planeOf`. `DogfightSnap` is now the plane's state itself (it was `{ you }`). `openDogfightWorld`'s `fetch`, `attachDogfight`'s `worlds`.
- **Rejected alternatives:**
  - **Decoding chunks in worker threads:** a chunk decodes in 0.16 ms on the main thread, and gunzip already runs off it. A pool would need a second bundled entry point and transfers, to save less than a millisecond a chunk.
  - **Diffs against what each page acknowledged:** a baseline per page means a message per page. On a reliable, ordered connection, a group's previous message is a baseline every page in step with the group has.
  - **A per-page view rectangle from the page's rotation:** an isometric screen is close to a diamond on the grid in every rotation (1280 × 720 at 32 × 16 px is 40 by 45 tile widths along x − y and x + y), so `diamond` needs nothing from the page.
  - **Writing objects by chunk and coarse heights on every build:** they serve only clients holding part of a world, so they're optional layers added by their own commands, as the routes are.
- **Later:**
  - Committing objects by chunk and coarse heights for the cities' fixtures: about 8 MB for nyc.
  - The view's bake reading objects by chunk.
  - Snapshots by type (several schemas in a room).
  - A deadline on a chunk fetch (D-120).
  - The rover bench's own snapshot in binary.

### D-124: Runtime world edits, first slice: the level's placed objects taken out and put back, the tiles they touch baked again, and Fogfront's wardens knocking down the Presidio's buildings (accepted)
- **Why:** D-009 anticipated player edits re-baked locally and D-111 left "edits as operations a game can send" for later. Fogfront (D-121) showed what that costs today: its matches place their own buildings as entities and keep their own grid, while the level's buildings and trees can't be touched. The smallest edit that opens up play is taking a placed object out of the world (and putting it back), in every page's view and in the server's queries at once.
- **What:**
  - **`WorldEdits`** (`view.edits`, and `openWorld(…).edits` for a server, no baking there): `remove(objects)`, `restore(objects?)` (every one by default), `apply(op)`, `isRemoved`, `removed()`, `list()` (the ops that changed something, each with only the objects it changed, to send and replay), `version` and `onChange`. An object is named by **`ObjectHit.index`**, its place in the objects file, stable for a package: instances have no ids of their own, and the package hash already keys everything else to that file. An op naming indices the world hasn't, or not an op at all, changes nothing (ops come over the network).
  - **The queries agree at once:** the `ObjectIndex` leaves removed objects out of `objectsAt` and `landmarks` (a landmark loses a removed part, and goes with its last).
  - **The tiles an edit touches:** those whose bake builds the object (`objectInSlice`, now shared by the bake host and the view): its anchor in the tile's slice (its cells and the context margin, 16 cells at render scale 1, 8 on micro-terrain), or its big footprint reaching into it; the tiles looked at span the anchor as well as the footprint, since a small footprint's anchor may lie outside it (Paris, Hong Kong and Tokyo have such objects). That is every tile its pixels, shadow and ambient occlusion fall in, since a bake only ever drew it from those. A `TileEdits` map keeps each tile's removed objects, which drives three things: the bake message (`BakeTileMessage.removed`, echoed in `TileResult.removed`), the cache key, and what an op makes out of date.
  - **The workers stay stateless about edits:** the host leaves a tile's removed objects out before meshing (`objectsFor`), and meshes a kept scene again when its removed list differs from the one it was meshed without. A removed building's cells still keep the decorative scatter off, so the scatter is the same in every tile and no trees sprout in a razed lot. The bare variant (D-079) needs nothing more: it hides what the scene has.
  - **No blinking:** in the rotation shown, an edited tile's image stays on the GPU, marked stale, until its new bake replaces it (`ready()`, `tilesPending` and the idle baking treat it as not baked); in the other rotations, and for bare variants there, the images are let go and bake when turned to. A bake that was under way when an edit came in is known by its echoed list, drawn only where nothing newer is, and asked for again (a tile that failed to prepare or bake isn't: it made nothing). A tile let go forgets its ground variant too, so turning back shows its ground first rather than a hole.
  - **The cache:** an edited tile is kept under its removed objects (`…|edits:12,40`: the list itself, short for one tile, so two lists never share a key), never the plain key; with them put back the plain key finds the old tile again (live: the restore came from the cache, pixel for pixel the image from before).
  - **`query.pickObject(screenX, screenY)`:** the placed object drawn under a canvas point, or null where the view ray meets the ground first. It shares `pick`'s ray, marches down from the highest object top (`ObjectIndex.highest`) to the ground in eighths of a step, and takes the first object whose footprint and estimated height the ray is inside; an object without a top (a tree, a prop) reaches 3 steps over its base at render scale 1, as the web inspector's `objectsOnRay` takes it. Clicking a tall building's face used to pick the ground behind it.
  - **Fogfront:** the play area's buildings (`building.*`, 400-odd, mostly 1–4 cells and under 2 steps) are `Structure`s with hit points `50 + 40 · cells walled + 60 · height in steps` (130–560, at most 1000); landmarks, walls and bridges stay walls. `buildGrid` also keeps every object walling each cell. A right click with wardens on a building (`pickObject`) sends `{ t: "raze", u, obj }`; wardens walk toward the walled cell nearest them (the middle may be a U's open courtyard, out of reach), strike when within reach of a cell it walls, and give up where a path gets no nearer. A building at 0 falls: the match's grid opens the cells nothing else standing walls (paths and building places open up), and its object goes on the match's `razed` list, which the shared snapshot carries, so a page that comes late gets it in its first one. Each side's snapshot has the buildings it sees knocked about (`hurt`). The page puts the list into `view.edits` and its own cells (`Razed.sync`: a list that doesn't go on from the last, a new match, puts everything back first), drops dust where a new one fell and leaves rubble (an entity: a slab and a heap on each cell it walled). Each room's match now copies the level's cells: they were shared between rooms, which was harmless only while nothing changed them.
  - Two test fakes gained `index` and `pickObject`.
- **Rejected alternatives:**
  - **Telling each worker the removed set (a message of its own):** a bake already under way, a worker that started later, and the order of messages between workers would all need handling; a list per tile in the bake message keeps a worker's state the scenes it keeps.
  - **Skipping the cache for edited tiles:** a razed lot is drawn again on every visit, and putting things back would bake what is already kept.
  - **A hash of the removed list in the cache key:** 32 bits can collide, and a collision would show another edit's tile for good.
  - **Hiding removed objects at bake time (`hide`, as the bare variant does):** they would still cast shadows and darken the ground's ambient occlusion where they stood.
  - **An id per object in the objects file:** a format change and a pipeline run for every package, for what the index gives for free while a package's hash names its file.
  - **Fogfront's razed buildings as per-side state:** the level is the same for everyone; only the hit points of buildings knocked about go through the fog of war.
- **Objects by chunk (D-123), met when main was merged in:** a server opening a package with `objectChunks` indexes each chunk's file on its own, and a chunk's file doesn't say an object's place in the whole objects file, which is what an edit names. Those hits say `index` −1 (`ObjectIndex`'s `named`), and edits leave them; the landmarks' file is the same. `openWorld`'s `EditLog` is sized from the manifest's object count. San Francisco's levels have no object chunks, so Fogfront is untouched. Naming them takes each instance's place in the whole written into the chunk files (`object_chunks.py`), or an id per object.
- **Later (terrain edits, not in this slice):**
  - **Where they would live:** an edit layer over the `ChunkStore` (sparse cell overrides for `base`, `shape`, `surface`, `water`, `road`, `roadClass`, `roadWidth`, lanes), applied in `slice()` and `cell()`, so the bake's slices and the queries (`heightAt`, `surfaceAt`, `roadAt`, `pick`) and `openWorld` agree without the workers knowing; ops by cell rectangle, and order-dependent (a raise then a set isn't a set then a raise), so `list()` replays in order rather than as a set.
  - **Heights and slopes:** a cell's corners are shared with its neighbours (`shape`), so an edit marks the slices that reach one cell further, and the corner heights of its neighbours must stay consistent or the mesh tears. Each tile's height range (`TileGrid`, from the manifest) and the view's depth range (`worldDepthRange`, from the package's height range) bound what a tile may hold: an edit above or below them needs both widened, or clamping. Objects re-base on edited ground (their bake reads `heightAt`), and the `ObjectIndex`'s cached bases have to be dropped for them.
  - **Micro-terrain (D-036):** the corners are 4–16 a cell side and the GPU builds the geometry from height textures (`buildTerrainField`); an edit is a stamp into those corners, the steep-rock and shores rules (D-094) follow from them, and the margin is 8 cells.
  - **Water:** a level per cell, shores drawn as planes where the package sinks the micro-terrain under it; flooding or draining needs neighbours' shores re-derived, and the queries' `waterLevel`.
  - **Surfaces:** cheap for the bake (material tables are per surface id), but the decorative scatter follows the surface, so the scatter ring around the tile changes too.
  - **Roads:** a road's mask names its neighbours' directions, so an edit is a pair of cells; lanes, classes and real widths follow, and the routes (`routes.json`) traffic and the example game's graded decks read would need the same edit, or traffic drives through.
  - **Objects:** placing new ones (a game's buildings in the world's own grid rather than as entities, D-121's "Later") is the same machinery with instances added after the file's: an index past the file's count.
- **Checked:** engine unit tests: the log (ops with only what changed, replay onto another world, bad ops ignored), `objectsAt` and `landmarks` leaving out removed objects and putting them back, `pickObject` finding a 300 m tower before the ground behind it in each rotation (and a tree, and nothing on open ground or once the tower is removed), the tiles an edit touches (an anchor near tile edges, a bridge reaching four tiles across, each tile's removed list as ops come and go), and the cache key per removed list. In Node over `fixtures/sf`: a Presidio building taken out frees its cells in `objectsAt`, and comes back. Fogfront: a warden knocks down a building in `ceil(hp / 9)` blows, its cells open where nothing else stands, a cell two buildings wall opens with the second, runners and unknown buildings are refused, and a new match puts it back; a page's `Razed` puts the list into its edits and cells and starts over for a new match; the play area reads 400-odd buildings with their hit points; through the loopback over the Presidio, a building falling reaches both pages' worlds (`objectsAt` no longer answers there, the cell opens, the level's own cells untouched), a page that comes late takes it out from its first shared snapshot, and the next match puts it back everywhere. Live in headless Chromium on SwiftShader, on Fogfront's page: `view.edits.remove` of a building drawn on screen re-baked 2 tiles in 0.36–0.8 s each (mesh 72–76 ms, the rest the bake; the page's first 14 tiles took 0.7–1.1 s each, baking at once), `ready()` 0.35–0.94 s after the edit (three runs), 1428 pixels changed in the building's box, `pickObject` there answered nothing and then the building again once put back, and the restore came from the cache with not a pixel changed. Two pages in one room: the Kiln raised a lodge, trained two wardens and knocked down the two-cell building nearest its hearth (170 hit points) in 15 s; both pages took it out (`objectsAt` empty, its cells open, rubble standing), no page errors. A review of the change found a tile that failed to prepare asked for again every frame once edited, tiles missed where an object's anchor lies outside its footprint, a let-go tile's ground left done, wardens going for a U-shaped building's open middle, and a 32-bit hash in the cache key: fixed, with tests for the missed tiles and the U. 700 tests in the workspace.

### D-125: Games on a level's geography: cells, paths, lines of sight and viewsheds in the engine, the fog as a number, a veil layer and chained fog passes; Fogfront's sight, ground, garrisons, landmarks and night (accepted)
- **Why:** the owner asked for the RTS (D-121) to play off each level's own geometry and features, to enrich and stress-test the engine, and to see which parts belong in the engine or the multiplayer package. Fogfront's paths and grid were its own, its sight was a circle that ignored the level, and nothing in it knew the woods, the hills, the roads' classes, the level's buildings beyond walls, its landmarks, or the Golden Gate's fog the style draws.
- **What, in the engine** (additive; a changeset):
  - **Cells for games** (`readCells`, `engine/src/cells.ts`): a rectangle of a level read once from its queries into plain arrays: ground heights, water, what walls each cell (the level's buildings, walls and landmarks; not trees or props, which a game may choose otherwise), trees, each cell's highest top (an object's, or its base and 3 steps where the world gives none), surfaces, road classes, and the objects on each. `findPath` (A*, eight ways, no corner cut, straightened; a game's `cost`, `minCost` and `maxClimb`), `lineClear`, `canStep`, `walkable`, `nearestWalkable`; `lineOfSight` over the tops between two points (the cells the eye and the point are on don't hide); `viewshed` (each cell an eye sees within a radius, into a field of nearest-eye distances; a game's `reach` for fog or dark); `setRemoved` and `followEdits` (D-124): cells under objects taken out open and their tops drop, from the cells' own lists, so a server keeps a copy per room and razes its match's buildings without the world the rooms share. Fogfront's grid and paths moved onto them (its `MapGrid` is `Cells` with its own buildings).
  - **The fog as a number** (`fogSampler(world, query)`): the style's fog at a point, height and time, by day or night, from the same field, ceiling, drift and patch noise the fog pass draws (the patches taken under the point, not on their plane along the view ray), or null where the style has none. A server reads it through `openWorld`; a page gives its fog layer the server's clock (`FogLayer.set({ time })`), so both reckon the same fog.
  - **A veil over cells** (`veilLayer({ ramp, shade, levels })`, `set(rect, values)`): a game's field over a rectangle (0–255 a cell; beyond it, its edge's), dithered into the frame by the fog pass toward a ramp's shade, pixel by pixel from where each stands, so buildings, trees and units stand in it as the ground does. The fog's lookup (`fogLut`) makes the darkening; levels 0.3, 0.55, 0.8 in Fogfront.
  - **Fog passes chained:** a frame ran the first screen pass any layer offered; now each layer's runs in turn (one `FogPass` a layer id, two targets taking turns), a fog field may start at any cell (`FogField.x0`, `y0`, a uniform in the pass), and a fogged pixel keeps its depth for the pass after it. The style's fog over a game's veil both show (live: the Tide's hearth, known but unseen, darkened under the drifting Golden Gate fog).
  - The size budget for every entry point, untree-shaken, rose to 115 KB (111.8 KB with D-124 and these; `createWorldView` alone 87.3 KB of its 90).
- **What, in Fogfront** (D-121's demo):
  - **Sight from the level:** each side's eyes (units 1.2 steps up; its buildings from their roofs, 6 and 4 steps; landmarks it holds; garrisons from the building's roof) run the engine's viewshed over the ground, the level's buildings and the woods' canopies, every 5 ticks: woods and hills hide what is behind them. An eye standing over the ground three cells round sees further (0.75 cells a step, up to 3); fog where the eye or the cell stands takes up to 60% of sight away (the fog over the cells sampled every 2 s); a unit standing in woods is seen only from 2 cells. A blow from a step or more above lands 30% harder. The page works out its own side's sight the same way four times a second from its snapshot and draws it as the fog of war (seen clear, seen before 59% veiled, never seen fully), with the style's fog drawn on the server's clock.
  - **Ground and roads:** the going on each cell by its road's class (motorway and trunk 1.7, primary 1.6, secondary and tertiary 1.5, residential 1.4, unclassified 1.3, living streets 1.2) or its surface (the level calls Baker Beach's sand `desert`: 0.8; scrub and rock 0.85; sand 0.7), trees 0.6 of that; A*'s guess at 1/1.7.
  - **Garrisons in the level's buildings:** units go in (G then a click, or a right click with runners alone): two a cell the building covers, at most eight, of one side; inside they are hidden from the other side and watch from its roof, 2 cells further; U over it brings them out beside it, and if it is knocked down they come out of the rubble with 15 damage. A count in the side's colour stands over each.
  - **Landmarks to hold:** the map widened to 144 × 110 cells (x 118–262, y 140–250) to take in the Palace of Fine Arts and the Golden Gate Bridge's shore end. Each landmark reaching in becomes an objective at the cell units can reach nearest its part nearest the map's middle (a flood from the Kiln's hearth: the first choice for the bridge was a cell cut off by the bluffs), named by the example game's gazetteer (`landmarkName`). A side alone within 3 cells of one for 10 s takes it (contested it stands still; left, the taking fades at half speed); it pays half a glim a second and gives an eye 4 steps over it seeing 9 cells. Who holds what is in the shared snapshot (famous places: no fog hides them), with the taking; a diamond in the holder's colour and a ring filling as it is taken on the page, a line on the board, a word to every page when one changes hands.
  - **Night:** on the match's clock, 240 s of day and 90 of night (`RTS_DAY=day,night` on the server to try it quicker); units see 60% as far by night (buildings are lit, and see as by day), the fog takes its night strength, and the page switches to the style's night palette with its lit windows. The shared snapshot says night and when the light next changes; the board counts down to dusk or dawn.
- **What belongs where** (the owner's question):
  - **Moved into the engine now:** the cell rectangle and everything over it (paths, lines walked, lines of sight, viewsheds, edits applied), the fog as a number, the veil layer, chained passes. Any game on a level wants them, server or page, and none of them knows a match.
  - **Stays in the game:** what a cell costs to cross and what walls it, sight radii and bonuses, garrison and objective rules, night's effects, what a building's hit points are.
  - **For the engine later:** landmark names in the package (`LandmarkInfo.name`, from the registry; the demo borrows the example game's gazetteer, D-051 keeps names out of the engine today); a viewshed by shadow casting (a ray a cell is O(r³) an eye: 2 sides × ~15 eyes × radius 9 is a few ms every 5 ticks); cells that follow terrain edits once those exist (D-124 "Later"); the veil's field as a texture updated in place rather than made anew each time (15,840 bytes four times a second here).
  - **For the multiplayer package later:** snapshots per team, serialized once for its players, and for spectators with a team's sight or all of it (D-121 "Later"; a veil makes watching a side worth having); sending what changed (sight makes snapshots smaller, not their unchanged parts).
- **Rejected alternatives:**
  - **Line of sight on the GPU (a shadow map from each eye):** exact to the pixel, but the server has no GPU, and a side's sight must be the same on the server, which decides what each page is sent, and on the page, which draws it.
  - **The veil drawn as sprites or an SVG overlay:** flat, and buildings and trees would not stand in it; the fog pass already darkens by where each pixel is in the world.
  - **Trees as walls for sight:** units in woods would see nothing at all; canopies hide what is behind them, and from within a wood one sees out of it.
  - **Objectives at a landmark's anchor:** the Golden Gate Bridge's is over the water; the cell to stand at must be one units can reach.
  - **Night as a separate fog:** night changes the palette the style already has; sight is the game's.
  - **Fog and sight per tick:** the fog drifts a third of a cell a second; sight every 5 ticks (0.25 s) keeps the server's tick near 1 ms with the Presidio's 15,840 cells.
- **Later:** maps from any level (start points, seeps and objectives chosen from the geography, and checked for reach, as done here by hand; the Strip's or Manhattan's grids, Kyoto's temple grounds); trains and ferries as neutral movers (the routes have them, but none crosses the Presidio; the server would run the traffic, D-111's "traffic around several foci, fed by the server"); a minimap, control groups, rally points and a ranged unit; the bot holding landmarks and using garrisons; a fog of war that remembers what it saw of the other side's buildings (it shows them as now).
- **Checked:** engine unit tests of the cells (reading water, walls, roads, trees and tops; razed cells opening and following edits; paths round walls and water, at a game's costs, within a climb, and to the nearest reachable cell; lines of sight over low ground but not through a wall or a ridge, and over both from high up; a viewshed with a wall's shadow, a second eye filling in, a reach cutting it short); the veil (nothing until set, its field and origin, a darkening at each level, wrong sizes and ramps refused); the fog sampler over `fixtures/sf` (thick in the Golden Gate, none downtown or over the ceiling, drifting over minutes, none in the desert's style). Fogfront: a building hides what is behind it until it falls; high ground sees further; a unit in woods is seen only from near; fog shortens sight; a blow from above lands 1.3 times; night shortens sight; units go into a building, hidden and watching further, so many and one side's, come out beside it, and are thrown out hurt when it falls; a landmark taken by a side alone, paying and seeing, contested standing still, and its taking fading; over the Presidio, pace by the level's ground (sand, woods, four road classes), the fog over part of the map and drifting, both landmarks reachable from both hearths; every D-121 and D-124 test still passing on the engine's cells. A review of the change found a garrison's own roof hiding the ground beyond it (lines of sight now leave out cells the eye looks out over, `ignore`), an order to go in answering "the other side holds it" from anywhere on the map (a way to find hidden garrisons: now only on arrival), units brought out into a courtyard or onto a wall (now where they went in), a move off the map leaving a unit searching for a path every tick at the edge (moves now kept inside, and a path to a goal off the rectangle ends at its edge), and, smaller, a layer's fog pass kept after the layer went, the fog over the cells up to 2 s stale at dusk and dawn, objectives flooded from a fixed cell, a rival's taking not fading while the holder stood by, and a unit thrown out of a fallen building at no hit points left a step longer: fixed, with tests. 725 tests in the workspace. Live on the built production server in headless Chromium on SwiftShader: the fog of war around the Kiln's base (188 of 15,840 cells seen at the start), the Tide's known hearth darkened under the drifting fog; G and a click sent three runners 17 cells into the Presidio's main post (in within 7 s, a 3 over it, the area round it seen from its roof: 224 cells, 187 before the roof stopped hiding the far side), U brought them out; with 40 s days, night fell (the night palette, lit windows, the board counting to dawn, 165 cells seen against 187); no page errors.

### D-128: Fogfront's maps from any level: hearths and seeps chosen from a level's cells and checked for reach, the map a room's setting, and maps on Manhattan, Kyoto, Kowloon and the Strip (accepted)
- **Why:** the owner wants Fogfront (D-121, D-125) to exercise every level's geography. Its map was one hand-placed rectangle of the Presidio, with hearths and seeps placed by hand and checked by hand (D-125's "Later": maps from any level).
- **What:**
  - **A map** (`maps.ts`) is a committed level, a rectangle of it, and a name: `presidio` (`sf`, the hand-placed layout as it was), `midtown` (`nyc-walk`, 160 × 160 cells at 10 m), `higashiyama` (`kyoto-walk`, 155 × 145), `kowloon` (`hongkong-walk`, 138 × 150) and `strip` (`southwest-walk`, 150 × 140 at 20 m), each 15,000–26,000 cells like the Presidio's 15,840.
  - **The generator** (`planMap`, over the map's cells as `buildGrid` reads them; 40–90 ms) is the same each time for a level and a rectangle. First the map's largest walkable region (`mainRegion`, flooded once over the cells as paths step), where everything goes, so each reaches every other. Then the **hearth sites**: a 3×3 footprint on open (no woods), dry, level ground (`MAX_RISE`), with every cell round it walkable, so a hearth closes no way; the roomiest in each 8-cell block, counting open cells within 4 of its footprint. Then the **pair**: the roomier one, a cell nearer or further than half the map's diagonal costing as much as a cell less of room, and nearer the map's middle on a tie; under 0.8 of that distance only when nothing else fits. Then the **seeps**: three by each hearth, 5 cells' walk from it and further from the other's by more than that, the furthest from the other hearth first (behind the base), 3 cells apart; and two between, a cell's walk at most nearer one hearth than the other, on or by the shortest way, the second at least a fifth of the hearths' walk from the first. Walks are measured by `walkFrom`, a flood that weighs each step by the cell's pace as A* does (roads quicker, woods slower), so each side's seeps are as far as the other's by time, not just by steps: by steps alone, Kowloon's second middle seep was 15 cells further for one side once roads were taken.
  - **Objectives:** the landmarks reaching into the map, as before, at the reachable cell nearest the part nearest the middle, now in the largest region rather than reached from the Kiln's hearth. Two more rules: a landmark that no cell's middle meets (the High Roller's thin rim) is taken from its anchor (`query.landmarks()`), and one whose spot is within twice the holding radius of an earlier one counts once (Higashiyama's pair of Gion torii, Kiyomizu-dera's hall beside its pagoda: eight became six).
  - **The map as a room's setting:** the lobby takes `{ map }` (an unknown one is refused), and quick play puts a page in a waiting room on the map it asked for (`LobbyOptions.fits` now also gets the settings the page asked for, as `settings` checked them: additive in the package). The server opens each map's level once, when a room first plays it. The shared snapshot and the lobby's info carry the map. A room opened by naming it plays the Presidio.
  - **The page:** `?map=` picks it (the invite link carries it), the view loads its level, with whichever city packs its style names (sf, nyc, kyoto, tokyo, hongkong: each only a worker's URL until a bake needs it), and the camera, cells and veil follow its rectangle. A page whose room plays another map loads itself again on the room's. The production build copies the five levels (21 MB). The page's script is 305 KB.
  - **A page joining a running room:** the room holds what comes before the page's handler (D-123), so the shared snapshot came in as the page's client was made, before what takes it was declared, and the page died. It now waits until the page is set up. This came with the merge of D-123 under Fogfront, not with the maps.
- **How each map plays** (the bot against an idle side, and against itself, in game seconds; ticks 0.4–0.8 ms on average; lodge places within reach of each hearth):
  - **Presidio:** 168 s and 240 s; 60 and 202 places (the Kiln's beach is narrow).
  - **Midtown:** 147 s and 282 s; 126 and 153. Half the cells are buildings (3,657 that wardens can knock down), so the walkable region (46%) is the streets, and sight runs down the street canyons. The Kiln's hearth stands in Bryant Park. Dense, but it plays.
  - **Higashiyama:** 156 s and 608 s; 77 and 167. The east is steep, wooded hills (heights up to 89 steps) that walls and woods mostly shut off; the play is in the temple lanes below. Six objectives, the most of any map.
  - **Kowloon:** 140 s and 432 s; 118 and 167. A third of the map is harbour (region 38%). The `hongkong-walk` level is Kowloon, low and flat (heights to 15 steps): its "hills" are Kowloon Park and the small rises, not Hong Kong Island's.
  - **The Strip:** 150 s and 709 s; 251 and 315. The casinos are great walled blocks in open desert lots, the roomiest map; the bot against itself takes longest. Its 20 m cells make a walk across it twice as far in metres.
  - None plays badly enough to drop. The bot holds no landmarks yet (D-125's "Later"), so they don't decide these matches.
- **Trains and ferries** (noted only, D-125's "Later"; `routes.json` against each rectangle): Kowloon has the Star Ferry's routes across the harbour (13 segments, 507 cells in the map); the Strip has one train segment (79 cells). Midtown has none in the routes (the subway is underground), nor do Higashiyama (the Keihan line runs by the Kamo, west of the map) or the Presidio.
- **Big worlds (D-123):** none of the five levels has object chunks (`objectChunks`, southwest and southwest-fly only), so their objects keep their indices, which the level's buildings and the world's edits need.
- **Rejected alternatives:**
  - **The farthest pair of sites:** on the Strip it put the hearths in opposite corners, a 235-cell walk apart (the Presidio's stand 94 cells apart in a straight line).
  - **Walks by steps (8-way breadth-first):** a diagonal counts as a straight step, and roads aren't quicker, so seeps "as far from either" weren't by time.
  - **The page asking the lobby for a room's map before joining:** private rooms aren't listed, and the room's own word is the one that counts; loading the page again on a mismatch is one line.
  - **Choosing each map's rectangle by a search over the whole level:** the rectangle is a designer's choice (what landmarks it takes in); the generator does the placing, which is what has to be checked.
- **Later:** the bot taking landmarks and using garrisons; a minimap, since the walk levels' maps are larger in metres; more maps (Paris, Tokyo, the Presidio's generated layout as another map); trains and ferries as neutral movers run by the server (the Star Ferry would cross Kowloon's harbour); the map in a room named without the lobby (a page's join could carry it).
- **Checked:** Node tests over the committed levels, served over HTTP from `fixtures/` (`rts-maps.test.ts`): on each map, the generator's hearths on open, level ground with a way round them; every seep, landmark and the other hearth reached by a path from both hearths (the Presidio's hand-placed layout as well); each side's own seeps as far a walk as the other's, the middle two within a cell; the same plan twice; each map's landmarks (Midtown's four, Kowloon's three, the Strip's two with the High Roller, Higashiyama's at least six apart); the bot taking an idle page's hearth on Midtown and on the Strip through the loopback, the shared snapshot and the lobby's info saying the map; and the lobby over HTTP: quick play on Kowloon twice to one room, the Presidio to another, an unknown map refused, and a page in each room hearing its map once the server has opened that level. Every D-121, D-124 and D-125 test still passing. Live on the built production server in headless Chromium on SwiftShader: on Midtown, a page by quick play waited and a second on its invite link joined in 25 s, a side each, both seeing Midtown's four landmarks and their own hearths; a page with only the room's code loaded itself again on Midtown; on the Strip against the bot, playing in 5 s, the Paris tower and the High Roller on the board, 29 frames a second; no page errors after the fix above (before it, the page joining the running room threw on every shared snapshot). 777 tests in the workspace.
